Expecting a 50bp Fed ease next week; limited passthrough to other CBs

Global core inflation remains sticky but goods price pressure may be fading

• Asian export lift looks idiosyncratic as global IP softens

0 Next week: CBs deliver a differentiated set of outcomes and guidance

## Swing away

In assessing the coming path of DM policy rates through the lens of risk management, we have emphasized three important points. First and foremost, the balance of US risks has shifted substantially this quarter as inflation pressure eases while job growth materially slows. A shift in focus toward the labor market side of the dual mandate seen in recent Fed communications points to a break from the FOMC’s mid-year gradualism guidance. We look for 100bp of policy rate cuts in relatively short order, with next week’s meeting delivering a front-loaded 50bp move. Second, the US stands alone in delivering strong supply-side performance while also facing elevated recession risk. We thus do not expect a similar break from gradualism elsewhere, a point reinforced by recent communications from Western European central banks. Finally, there is significant two-sided risk around the 2025 rate path in which a tightly packed consensus forecasts an additional 100bp of US easing. This outcome is reasonable if inflation returns to target amid sustained positive supply side performance. But there is also a material risk that is followed by labor demand rebounds and inflation proves sticky, stalling Fed easing. Indeed, the easing in financial conditions associated with the successful front-loading of cuts will likely increase the risk of such an outturn.

Better US inflation readings are contributing to the shift in risk as they calm the Fed’s fears arising from the 1Q24 spike. But inflation news, by itself, is not sufficient to warrant a discrete step-down in rates next week. Indeed, looking through this year’s up-and-down readings, core CPI and PCE inflation both rose at about a 3%ar in the first eight months of the year. Instead, recent labor market

Malcolm Barr (44-20) 7134-8326malcolm.barr@jpmorgan.com

developments likely have the most impact on the Fed. Aligned with Governor Waller’s recent assessment, we do not view the triggering of the Sahm rule as a recession signal when other indicators do not point to a significant shift toward labor shedding (Figure 1). Rather, the unemployment rate aligns with a range of indicators pointing to the labor market moving into better balance due to strong supply and moderating demand. For the Fed, this reduces concerns that easing in the face of tight labor markets could embed persistent pressure on wage and service price inflation. Until recently, the rebalancing of US labor markets took place against moderating but still strong labor demand. Now the slowing in job growth is more abrupt. As we’ve said it is unusual for private sector job growth to slow below a 1%ar within a sustained expansion phase, and the August employment report (including revisions) is now touching this level (Figure 2). While not pointing to a slide into recession, the elevated risk signaled by recent job reports provides the most compelling reason to ease 50bp next week.

That the Fed has not tipped its hand, and markets are undecided about next week’s outcome, makes sense. For a “consequential” first step of this magnitude, Chair Powell will likely need to build a consensus within an FOMC where many members likely remain biased toward caution. Discussions are ongoing and will probably incorporate an assessment following next Tuesday’s retail sales report, with the outcome remaining a close call. But regardless of next week’s decision, we expect a shift away from gradualist risk management to be reflected in both committee projections and Chair Powell’s press conference. With relatively little change to next year’s economic projections, we expect the median FOMC member to project more than 200bp of easing through the end of 2025, up from a projection of 125bp at the June meeting.

## European central banks remain cautious

The ECB cut its deposit rate by 25bp as expected and narrowed the corridor in line with the recent changes to its operational framework. Most importantly, the policy statement, press conference, and new staff forecasts all confirmed the ECB remains on a path of gradual rate normalization, guiding toward quarterly 25bp cuts. The door to an October move is not closed but will require a significant rise in growth concerns. As that meeting is only five weeks away, we suspect only a sharp drop in the September PMI could bring it into play. The PMI’s decline must go well beyond an expected post-Olympics slide in France, however, and we remain comfortable with our call of the next 25bp cut being in December.

A similar message of gradualism should come from the Bank of England, which we expect to remain on hold next week and guide toward a November ease. BoE communication has turned more dovish recently, focusing on survey-based and forward-looking indicators. But underlying wage and price data remain strong, and growth looks resilient. The jobs market is also holding up and there are renewed signs of housing activity. On QT, the BoE has given no clear indication on the decision, creating more uncertainty, but we maintain our outof-consensus call for a step up in balance sheet reduction.

We also expect Norges Bank to be on hold next week, and we are backing off our expectations for a dovish pivot because of currency weakness. As Norges Bank normally flags a rate cut one forecast meeting ahead, we have pushed back our projection for an initial ease to March 2025. While we have viewed the Riksbank as the DM central bank most likely to join the Fed in delivering a 50bp easing, the data flow has not increased pressure on the bank and we maintain our baseline forecast for a series of 25bp cuts at upcoming meetings.

## BoJ to reinforce rate hike signal

Although the BoJ will almost certainly keep its policy settings unchanged at next week’s meeting, we believe it remains on track to tighten policy again this year. Economic data through 3Q showed continued recovery in private consumption supported by real wage gains and improving confidence. We believe this sets the stage for a virtuous cycle enabling inflation to move back up to the 2% target on a sustained basis. Against this backdrop, board members have consistently signaled a move up in policy rates to at least 1% next year. Market pricing has yet to fully embrace the BoJ’s policy guidance. We thus expect the BoJ will continue to signal additional rate hikes as it seeks to align rate expectations with macro fundamentals.

## Waning goods price pressure…

August CPI data are tracking our forecast for a limited moderation in 2H24 global core inflation amid continued sticky services inflation. At the same time, the building good price pressures seen in 1H24 have started to wane. (Figure 3). A 15% tumble in crude oil prices over the past two months has been accompanied by a retracement in global shipping costs. More important than the pass through to consumer prices is

the identification issue related to demand and supply impulses behind these moves. If we are right that supply dynamics explain much of the waning pressure, then any passthrough to lower inflation should support household purchasing power. However, the cooling in global manufacturing surveys and softening in China demand may be sending a more worrying signal about growth momentum as we move through 2H24.

## …widens policy space for EM CBs

Falling energy prices and Fed easing is a favorable combination for many EM central banks. But the rate sensitivity of these developments varies. The response will likely be greatest in EM Asia, where easing food and energy prices has already returned inflation to central bank targets. Policymaker concerns around FX and financial instability will be calmed if our frontloaded Fed call materializes, providing most space in the Philippines and Indonesia to bring forward cuts. In LatAm, domestic conditions are less conducive to frontloaded cuts given ongoing political and policy risks, but we still see ample space for cuts in Colombia and Mexico alongside more limited easing in Peru and Chile. In the CEE region, a localized resurgence in food inflation along with sticky above-target core inflation is likely to keep CBs more cautious. We expect the CNB and NBH to stick to a slower (25bp) pace of easing given also the large amount of cuts already delivered, and the NBP and NBR to stay on hold through year-end.

Figure 4: China IP and real GDP
![](images/3618ed8e8cb6c4ce2251458120e2ed84376c9613386d7d0baddbca7322f17d04.jpg)

## China: Deflation nation

Early indicators for August point to building deflationary pressures in China. Despite rising food prices, core consumer prices are unchanged in August and up just 0.3% over the past year. More upstream prices are already in deflation, with producer prices now down 1.8% from the prior year. With excess capacity building, business have little pricing power— damping earnings potential and further weighing on sentiment and spending. We expect August activity data to highlight still weak domestic demand with export driven gains and IP growth providing the main source for modest 4%ar 2H24 GDP gains (Figure 4). Policy has been steadily easing but the actions are still incremental. There are, however, a number of potential catalysts for a more meaningful policy

shift: (1) September/October macro data suggest the economy’s weak links (housing and consumption) turn even weaker and fiscal revenue continue to contract; (2) a more aggressive shift in tone at the Politburo meeting and annual Central Economic Work Conference by November/December; (3) a Trump victory in the election that kicks off a new trade war.

## Less signal from Asian export lift

Concerns about the global industrial cycle contrast with manufacturing strength across EM Asia, reflected in both PMIs and exports. North Asian export indicators have generally come in above expectations. While the manufacturing lift would traditionally be positive given the region’s lead in the cycle, there are reasons to fade the message this time. First, the latest smart phone cycle is providing a temporary lift that will likely fade later this year. Second, a recovery in auto parts production from earlier disruptions is also likely to fade. Third, some of the latest boost in EM Asia factory output owes to businesses wanting to get ahead of potential electionrelated disruptions that could delay holiday shipping. Limited domestic demand across Asia and signs of softening in global capex keep risks to the downside for 2H24 IP growth.

## Brazil: Swimming against the global tide

After a 9-month easing cycle starting in August 2023, Brazil’s BCB is set to embark on a tightening cycle as the Fed starts easing. Signals from board members have been mixed, but we and consensus expect BCB to hike 25bp to 10.75% next week amid robust economic growth and after recent FX intervention to stem BRL weakness. We expect a unanimous vote and a hawkish tone, but no pre-commitment to the pace or overall magnitude of the hiking cycle. Despite a firmer inflation outlook for 4Q, imminent Fed easing and some moderation in growth should help limit overall rate hikes to around 100bp (25bp per meeting), with a likely return to easing by 3Q25.

## SARB to kick off easing cycle

South Africa’s reserve bank (SARB) has thus far downplayed an improving inflation outlook but is poised to deliver a 25bp cut next week. Headline inflation could drop almost 1%-pt point below target next quarter on the back of a drop in transportation costs and a strengthening in the rand. While a larger 50bp clip could be justified given the weak economy and favorable inflation profile, the MPC probably will stress elevated uncertainty around the global outlook and the rand. The recent implementation of the two-pot pension reform—which the SARB estimates will add 0.3%-0.7%-pt to growth and 0.1%-0.3%-pt to inflation next year—adds to uncertainty. We have incorporated an impact on the lower end of the SARB’s estimates and believe the SARB will cut rates to a broadly neutral 7.25% by mid-2025.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our estimate for 3Q24 global real GDP growth edged up this week to 2.1%ar (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster meanwhile moved sideways at 2.1%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth.

About 30% of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 3Q growth in 40% of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model is tracking a moderation in global capex growth to 1.1%ar after 2.9%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

We still see the fundamentals in place to support a resilient consumer, but early August final demand indicators are showing softness. Global (ex China) consumer confidence moved sideways at a depressed level, and momentum in global auto sales looks to have slowed to a stall. Our capex nowcaster also suggests slower growth in 3Q.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds rallied this week amid a focus on easing by DM central banks (ex BoJ). Ahead of the FOMC meeting, we continue to see a 50bp cut at the Sep and Nov meetings, and that even in the event of a 25bp cut Chair Powell’s tone in the press conference would likely signal a willingness to increase the pace if warranted by ongoing labor market softening. We continue to favour 3s/30s UST curve steepeners as a bullish proxy with a better carry profile than outright longs that should also be supported by rising term premia.

In the Euro area, the ECB cut policy rates by 25bp and signalled that it is minded to continue with a quarterly pace of cuts. We retain a bias to trade duration on the long side but do not find the risk-reward attractive in the near-term. We stay neutral duration outright, express a steepening bias via 5s/30s conditional bull steepeners the EUR swap curve, and hold longs in 10Y Spain vs. Germany. In the UK, we expect the BoE to stay on hold at its Sep meeting with little change in its forward guidance, and keep 5s/30s SONIA steepeners as a bullish proxy. In Japan, we see the BoJ focusing on its assessment of recent favorable domestic data and conditions for a next hike at its Sep meeting, and hold 2s/10s JGB curve steepens.

In EM, a resilient outlook for EM (ex China) growth, along with a slowing trend in global growth and inflation that we do not see as a precursor of a recession, mean that the backdrop should be supportive for EM local rates. We are OW EM local duration overall, with OWs in India, Czechia, Türkiye, the Dominican Republic and Uruguay partially offset by an UW in Chile as well as a short duration overlay (EMOS, Sep 9<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

Growth trends are more important than the Fed’s choice of 25bp vs. 50bp next week for US HG. It will be interesting to see if the actual Fed cut begins a period of even stronger demand for fixed income, as has historically been the case. Yields close to 5% remain historically attractive and flows could remain strong if rate volatility subsides post Fed. The next 7 weeks include the Fed, 3Q earnings reports, and the election, each potential drivers of an increase in volatility. We believe the technical and fundamentals are in place to keep HG bonds spreads range-bound near current levels, so long as the growth data holds up well, which is the JPM expectation (CMOS, Sep 13<sup>th</sup>). Historically, HY bonds and loans have performed well in the 3-month stretch following a first Fed rate cut in an easing cycle, albeit mixed on a 12-month basis (HY and LL Research, Sep 13<sup>th</sup>).

Euro credit cash had a soft week, with IG and HY 3bp and 10bp wider, respectively, although synthetic indices outperformed heading into the roll. The main source of weakness was in Autos, which widened by 12bp in IG and 30bp in HY, after a profit warning at BMW. We turned UW on the sector toward the end of last year, although cheaper valuations arguably now present an attractive entry opportunity in higherquality names. We take profit on our OW Bank T2 versus Corporate Hybrids trade, with the widest ratio between the asset classes since last October (European Credit Weekly, Sep 13<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

A lower range in yields is the main tradable theme in FX for now regardless of the Fed outcome next week. Lower yields are supportive of USD weakness vs. funders; but approach to high beta FX should be tactical with recession and trade policy risks not yet priced. FX carry remains vulnerable to lower yields. Several currencies haven’t caught down to US yields yet. Attractive trades mostly involve long JPY on crosses or short USD vs. funders. We discuss G10 central bank implications for FX given the flurry of meetings in the next two weeks.

G10 forecasts: Funders upgraded. CHF and JPY forecasts upped. EUR/CHF to test 0.90. USD/JPY put at 140 but further US labour market easing would increase downside risks.

EUR/CHF 0.90 (0.93). USD/JPY 2Q25 140 (144). EUR/USD near term unchanged at 1.05; 2Q 1.12; 3Q 1.15. EUR/GBP 0.85 (0.84). AUD/USD 0.67. EM: Turned OW Asia FX intraweek. Asia forecasts are mostly upgraded on lower US yields and narrowing rate differentials. USD/CNY unchanged (4Q 7.15). USD/KRW 1300 (1350). USD/INR 84.10 (83.90). USD/ZAR 4Q 17.65. USD/MXN 18.70 (17.75). USD/BRL 5.45 (5.20) (KCV, Sep 13<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

Weather subdues oil demand. Global oil demand is falling short of our estimates due to typhoon Yagi in Asia and hurricane Francine in the US Gulf Coast, which reduced regional travel. Despite strong US travel activity over Labor Day, global oil demand is tracking 1.13 mbd growth YTD, below our projected 1.5 mbd gain. Global observable oil inventories increased by 32 mb in the first week of September, offsetting nearly half of August’s draw. Crude stocks rose mainly in China, while oil product inventories increased significantly in the US. OECD commercial oil stocks grew in all regions except Europe, which saw a slight draw (Oil Demand & Inventory Tracker, Sep 12<sup>th</sup>).

Low natgas price is a cure for low price in 2025, weatheradjusted. We believe the window of opportunity to see strong 2025 Natgas production growth is closing. With lower prices for longer, we now do not expect substantial drilling activity increases until December of this year and have reduced our view of rig growth. We revise 4Q24 price to \$2.75/MMBtu but leave our 2025 price forecast of \$3.70/MMBtu in place with the belief that under normal weather, upward support for 2025 price remains from current levels (US Natural Gas, Sep 13<sup>th</sup>).

Grain inventories poised to decline further. US inventories of cotton, soybeans, and corn declined in September as expected, while wheat was unchanged. Globally, cotton and corn inventories also fell, but wheat inventories remained stable due to increased stocks in Canada and higher production in Australia and Ukraine. The US corn yield increased slightly, soybean yield held firm, and cotton yield dropped to a nine-season low. We stay long the agri complex via an index, and focus on sugar, wheat, and corn, as markets are discounting supply and geopolitical risks (Agricultural Markets Update, Sep 12<sup>th</sup>).

Copper substitution is a perennial risk, but is unlikely to fill the 2030 market deficit. Our analysis indicates that, although substitution thrifting could accelerate over the medium term, especially in newer technologies such as xEVs and renewables, we do not believe it will be sufficient to fill the long-term market deficit in copper. We therefore remain positive on long-term copper prices and equity exposure (Global

Copper, Sep 11<sup>th</sup>).

## Draghi on competitiveness in the EU: action needed

• Draghi offers a comprehensive diagnosis of competitiveness issues in the EU...

• ...and an important road map for the new Commission and European Parliament

• But lack of political consensus likely to stymie progress on key measures

• No material implications for near-term macro outlook

Mario Draghi, the former president of the ECB and prime minister of Italy, published a long-anticipated report on European competitiveness this week, calling for a new industrial strategy. Several reports before the summer were looking at related issues (the Letta report, the French Treasury report, the ESMA report). All have some recommendations in common, but the Draghi report takes a deeper dive into competitiveness. It identifies three areas of action: (i) the innovation gap with the US and China, (ii) a joint plan for decarbonization and competitiveness; and (iii) increasing securities and reducing dependencies.

In our view, the 400-page report diagnoses low EU productivity growth well and many of the recommended policies make sense. But that doesn’t mean they will be easy to implement or lead to quick growth returns. For example, reducing red tape for firms (especially SMEs), increasing innovation, further leveraging private capital for cross-border risk sharing, and upgrading workforce skills have been objectives for a while. Still, there are plenty of “micro” sectoral measures that could make a difference and warrant scrutiny.

In terms of the current political landscape, there has been a significant shift to the right in the EU. Political polarization has also increased, with France and Germany seemingly more inward looking. Moreover, the “frugals” are unlikely to support additional EU-level instruments unless there is clear evidence of the success of Next Generation EU (the pandemic recovery package). Capital markets union (CMU) faces the same bottlenecks that have stymied progress for many years.

Nevertheless, the Draghi report does provide a roadmap of priority issues for the new European Commission and Parliament. Overall, even if there are no material implications for the near-term macro outlook, it’s an important “call for action”. Given it will take time before all new Commissioners will be approved by the Parliament, serious work of the new Commission will only likely start in early 2025. One event that could increase the prospects of change (e.g., an EU-level defence instrument and further reform of industrial policy) would be a Trump victory in the US election.

## Key themes and proposals

Motivating the Draghi report is weak growth in Europe over the last two decades, continued divergence with the US, and the increasing challenges from China. The divergence in GDP growth that primarily reflects lower productivity growth can be seen in Figure 1. Investment has also been consistently lower in the EU (Figure 2), despite a higher rate of savings. Higher energy prices have played a role in this, with electricity prices 2-3 times higher in the EU relative to the US and China.

Figure 1: Output per hour worked
![](images/d8b46efb0686f2b68099718b18de03e566160bd2536c541ea5f2003cabfa23ca.jpg)

Figure 2: Gross fixed capital formation as a % of GDP
![](images/8ab09d46f732e748ca6ea33f8959e2ab5410eb4b3df96da2ad1c90929f90434e.jpg)
Source: BLS, Eurostat, J.P. Morgan

The report covers a lot of ground and lists many measures, including to increase innovation and skills. Below are a few key highlights:

Based on the latest Commission estimates, there are additional investment needs of EUR 750-800 billion per year (about 4.5 percent of EU GDP) between 2025-30. This is significantly larger than the Marshall Plan post-WWII, and as the report acknowledges over ¾ will have to come from the private sector. Areas of focus are energy and transport to help achieve the energy transition, digital technologies, defence, and innovation.

Various measures are proposed to further CMU, including: (i) ESMA gradually becoming the single common regulator for all EU securities markets; (ii) harmonizing insolvency frameworks; (ii) centralising clearing and settlement; (iii) making more second pillar pensions available to expand the universe of long-term savings products; and (iv) issuing a common safe asset.

EU-level instruments are proposed for grid and interconnector investments of common EU interest and for financing joint defence research and equipment procurement.

Reform of competition and state aid policy to incentivize innovation and future competition, including by expanding important projects of common European interest.

Creation of 28<sup>th</sup> regimes outside of existing 27 national legal frameworks. This could apply to grid or interconnector projects of common European interest or to innovative start-ups to provide a single digital identity valid throughout the EU. Effectively, rather than try and harmonize national legal frameworks, this would bypass them for some priority areas and create an “EU” framework.

## Few implications for near term EU outlook

There was already a modest shift from green to competitiveness policies taking place before the end of the previous European Commission and Parliament. This was because of the geoeconomic challenges emanating from the US and China, and in expectation of a political shift to the right following the trends seen in EU national and state elections.

In terms of specific focus points, increasing innovation and upgrading skills have been EU focal points for many years, but is hard in practice. Making further progress on CMU to leverage private capital and reduce dependence on bank financing (Figure 3) has been a priority item for recent European Council discussions and the subject of the other major recent reports. However, as we have noted before, the disagreements amongst member states that have stymied CMU progress before are still very prevalent (e.g., on harmonization of insolvency regimes and withholding taxes). The proposal for European Securities and Markets Authority (ESMA) to transform into a single common regulator for all EU securities markets seems like a hard sell, especially given the changes in the French government. President Macron has been a big supporter of further progress on CMU, but the new government is likely to focus more on domestic issues. So rapid progress will likely be hard to deliver, even more so without further progress on banking union.

Figure 3: NFC bank loans as % of corporate debt %, both scales
![](images/81179a3733593b8df8a6968f99b706ff31a8954aa0c91ec13cd6014d520c45bd.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

The Draghi report does emphasize completing banking union and makes a proposal for a separate “jurisdiction” for major cross-border European banks. But making significant progress on banking union has been challenging in recent years, especially given the banking sector is well capitalized (i.e., no imminent threat of a crisis to focus minds). Some progress has been made, however, in that the European Council agreed before the summer a mandate on the review of the crisis management and deposit insurance (CMDI) framework for banks. Following negotiations with the new European Parliament, an agreement on the CMDI package could be reached by the 1H2025. But much will remain to be done on banking union.

Meeting investment needs significantly above those supported by the Marshall Plan will also be extremely challenging. As the report acknowledges, over ¾ will have to come from the private sector. But given attractive incentives available elsewhere (e.g., the US), the public sector will have to provide incentives in the EU as well. The question is how given fiscal space is limited and with divergent views across member states regarding the need for further EU-level instruments. There is also the classic “chicken and egg” problem: increasing productivity will help ease the fiscal adjustment needed. But fiscal measures are also needed to help boost productivity. Which comes first? Or is there divine coincidence?

Other divisive areas across member states are competition policy and state aid. Indeed, many smaller member states are worried that any permanent reform of state aid will simply allow the larger member states to provide more financing to their own companies (in particular France and Germany).

So, notwithstanding clear threats to EU competitiveness, it’s hard to see rapid action in the near term given continued divisions among member states. In France, President Macron and his previous government were big supporters of further EU integration, whereas the new government is likely to be much more circumspect. Germany seems to be turning inward, with Finance Minister Linder already rejecting the idea of joint borrowing. We still hold the view that the US Election could be more consequential for EU-level action, particularly when it comes to industrial policy and creating an EU-level instrument for defence procurement and research.

Next step is for Commission President Von der Leyen to announce her new Commissioners (expected next week). These Commissioners will need to be approved by the European Parliament, which could take a few months. So the serious work will likely start in early 2025, when the Draghi report and US election ramifications can be internalized into the EU priorities for the coming years.

## Australia: Deficit déjà vu

Australia’s current account posted back to back deficits in 1H24, a throwback to the pre-pandemic years

• External supports through COVID have now reversed, meaning deficits are likely this year and next

International student caps will lower services exports by A\$13 billion in the next two years

Australia’s current account delivered back to back quarterly deficits in 1H24 as falling commodity prices and rising income debits weighed on the external sector. While a break from recent performance, current account deficits have historically been the norm with the sector running persistent deficits for the better part of the last six decades (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Current account composition
![](images/a241860c6e6b0d3b6fe5e8e400439092b75fdb0d8609c25de574b4390d654dd2.jpg)

At the time we viewed the shift into surplus from late 2018 as unusual given the drivers, namely the combination of surging commodity prices and positive net equity flows, with stronger commodity income in foreign-owned domestic mining assets yet to manifest as investment income debits. The onset of the pandemic extended and amplified these factors as resilient commodity prices, zero international travel (Australia normally runs a travel services deficit) and low income payments to foreign owners of Australian assets supercharged the current account further, and delivered much larger than anticipated surpluses.

In the past few years these supportive factors have gradually reversed to some extent, with key commodity prices such as iron ore running at multi-year lows, net debit payments tracking 50% higher than the pre-COVID average, and the services deficit is still 10x the 2019 level. These factors alongside the government’s recent decision to cap the number of international students from 2025 (discussed below) have led us to lower our current account forecasts for this year and next. We now expect the current account deficit to print at A\$25 billion in CY2024, while a larger deficit of A\$35 billion is forecast for 2025.

## Super power

Australia’s managed fund industry has grown swiftly in the past decade, largely owing to consecutive increases in the compulsory superannuation contribution rate which now requires employers to allocate 11.5% of an employee’s gross income into incumbered retirement savings. Alongside rising asset prices, this dynamic has seen superannuation funds under management more than double in the past decade to A\$3.8 trillion (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Superannuation assets
![](images/acc871dff7368079a0c122bbe4c202727be597df6e1e1f6bf23255d315f5b93b.jpg)

Given Australia’s financial markets have become somewhat saturated by superannuation FUM (ACGBs outstanding: A\$934 billion, ASX market cap: \$2.9 trillion) managers have increased allocations to offshore markets. This is evident in the burgeoning wedge between total FUM and the value of assets invested domestically (Figure 2). Fund managers now allocate \~40% of assets into offshore markets, up from onequarter just ten years with investment split between international equities (27% of total assets), international fixed income (9% of total assets) and unlisted infrastructure (3% of total assets).

Figure 3: Portfolio net debit flows A\$billion, 4-quarter rolling
![](images/d9a779c183f5d096dc73307819659494d02ed761a1081ba5543b17bf7c64ab45.jpg)

As a result portfolio-related net equity income flows (dividends) turned positive last quarter and are expected to drift higher given the balance of funds invested. In contrast, net fixed income flows (coupons) have become increasingly negative as higher interest rates, and the relatively high rate of foreign ownership have seen offshore coupon payment effectively double (Figure 3). Given the mix of bond ownership lower interest rates should result in less negative coupon flows, which all else equal should deliver some improvement in the current account. That said, we think think this story will unfold gradually and expect negative fixed interest flows to more than offset developments on the equity side, leaving the income account in negative territory.

## Positive and negatives of commodity prices

Sticking with the income account, the direct investment deficit has narrowed in recent quarters and is now running broadly in line with the pre-COVID average. The mining investment boom of the previous decade, combined with a high degree of foreign ownership, means income outflows on direct investment holdings are now dominated by the resources sector, The sector’s repatriated profits and reinvested earnings account for roughly two thirds of aggregate direct income flows. Unsurprisingly, these flows are closely correlated with commodity prices (Figure 4) with the upswing and subsequent decline in the terms of trade inflated/deflating income debits. While an elevated terms of trade is net positive for the external sector, the upside to the trade balance is partly offset by the leakage of profits to offshore entities.

Figure 4: Income debits and terms of trade Direct investment debits (A\$bn, 4-qrtr rolling sum)
![](images/4c62e7520650c979ab6c533a9d866d1a0eaf58efd944e0132ae45f0a38f29e88.jpg)

While still elevated by historical standards, price weakness across Australia’s key commodity exports means the terms of trade is now almost 20% below 2022’s highs (Figure 5). The terms of trade – mining capex relationship is clearly different from the early stages of the investment boom, with mining-related capex now driven more by maintenance activity to offset depreciation of the existing capital stock. Accordingly we think any further terms of trade strength is more likely to be reflected in higher repatriated profits rather than reinvested earnings, with the latter maintaining a relatively low beta to spot commodity prices.

Figure 5: Export and import price indexes
![](images/deed6aba4b770a06e8b4107f86f3998430417e037092772f0c444d679b8c6338.jpg)

## Trade balance biased lower

The goods trade surplus continued to moderate through 1H24, owing in large part to the decline in key commodity prices, most notably iron ore. Our commodity/equity analysts expect iron ore prices to stabilize/move higher from current levels meaning the goods trade balance should start to show signs of stabilization.

Figure 6: Education and personal travel balance A\$ billion, both axes
![](images/8c146790e1e6fa4409795fa4eecb79b691633d77f3d532aaadbc81efe7e71dfb.jpg)

The services balance is difficult to predict, though our bias remains skewed toward deterioration as education exports shift lower following the government’s decision to cap the number of international student visas at 270K. While only marginally below (\~3%) the pre-COVID average, this cap implies international student numbers will decline sharply relative to 2024 and in value terms is expected to lower education related exports by a cumulative \~A\$13 billion over the next few years. We expect a partial offset in personal travel debits which remain elevated and likely to move lower from current levels (Figure 6), but in net terms we expect the education adjustment to dominate with services an increasing headwind for the current account in the next eighteen months.

## China: How policy can support consumption

• China’s consumption share is low, especially services consumption

• Low consumption is due to weak disposable income and a high saving rate

• Fiscal transfers are helpful, but have limited and temporary impact

In our view, policy needs to boost income growth, revive the services sector, and stabilize the housing market

• Reducing inequality and improving social safety networks can lower the saving rate

China is currently an exception to global trends in its growth and inflation dynamics, facing deflationary pressure after the post-pandemic reopening (link). Deflation pressure comes from multiple sources, including soft global commodity prices and domestic food price declines among others. But key among them is weak domestic demand, or more specifically the imbalance between domestic demand and supply. The recovery of consumption has lagged behind the recovery in production (Figure 1). The resulting deflation (or low CPI inflation) is pernicious for corporate earnings, government finances, household income, and debt metrics (link).

Figure 1: China's supply-demand imbalance
![](images/8361b8b9c1a38eb98ccee1a88ffc0e39b2efa181f3c8de3f5a8f4a28e8536bee.jpg)

Policymakers and investors agree that weak domestic demand needs to be addressed. The Politburo meeting in late July and recent guidance from the State Council emphasized the need to boost consumption (link). This is not the first time. One year ago, the State Council issued guidance (including 20 measures) to support consumption, but so far implementation has been disappointing. Concrete measures to support consumption have been limited, and their impact is small.

The result is ongoing debate over which policies should be deployed to boost China’s consumption. Candidates include trade-in subsidies for durable goods (preferred and adopted by the government) and cash transfer to households. In our view, the key missing components are measures to increase household income and lower the household saving rate.

## Low consumption in China

Household consumption as share of GDP is much lower in China than most other countries in the world. In 2023, household consumption accounted for 39.2% of GDP in China, compared to 67.9% in the US, 53.0% in Japan, 49.3% in Germany, 60.3% in India and a 55% world average (Figure 2). Over time in China, household consumption fell from 47.0% in 2000 to 34.6% in 2010, then gradually recovered to the current level.

Figure 2: Household consumption as % of GDP
![](images/68bc57e68ea3bf6a1c46af0e148bb57c5235756884a36eef6ac2c7e39a5d23c0.jpg)

A low share of consumption does not mean China’s consumption growth is weak. In the 2000-2010 decade when China’s household consumption as % of GDP continued to decline, average annual consumption growth (in real terms) was 9.6%, the strongest in the world. Back then, overall GDP growth was even stronger (10.4% on average). In 2011-2023, consumption increased at an average annual pace of 7.8% in China, while real GDP growth decelerated to 6.5% (Figure 3). Hence, the weakness of consumption is in relative terms.

Figure 3: Household consumption growth in China
![](images/688435ffd6b30d15c8d27ed8a73b72543806525a86de88b358ad4a7cde9276cf.jpg)

Low consumption in China is attributable to two reasons. First, the share of household income (as % of GDP) is low. While the flow-of-funds data suggest China’s household disposable income is about 60% of GDP, not much lower than global peers, the data can be misleading in that it includes depreciation of fixed assets and housing. An alternative measure, household disposable income from the NBS household survey (a net income concept), is only 43.9% of GDP (Figure 4), in which wage income is only 24.7% of GDP. Second, the household saving rate is high. Based on household survey data, the household saving rate was as high as nearly 30% in pre-pandemic years, rising to a peak of 34% in 2020, moderating afterwards but still staying high at 31.7% in 2023.

Figure 4: Household disposable income
![](images/3dcdfa66cca1894eadaa559aaf580481adf3c618b8ea74a87edbb5e38f585bf6.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

In addition, a breakdown of China’s household consumption suggests the weak link is services consumption, which only accounts for 45% of total household expenditure in China, whereas services consumption accounts for 67% of household expenditure in the US and 56% in Japan. If measured by the share in GDP, China’s goods consumption is on par with advanced economies, and the gap is mainly due to low services consumption (a comparison with the US is in Figure 5). This could be in part due to under-pricing of services activity in China, but more importantly, service consumption tends to be a key driver for consumption growth when a country moves from being a middle-income to a high-income economy.

Figure 5: Household consumption in China vs. the US
![](images/7ea3730c83d5a2928e972bbd553661f1e0e1593e868347818adc174ab19192bb.jpg)

## Drivers of consumption

Back in 2018 (link), we examined the key drivers of China’s consumption. An updated analysis (Table 1) shows that household income remains the primary driver for consumption, and the coefficient is larger in 2009-2022 than in 2003- 2009. The impact of house prices on household consumption is similar to our prior estimates: a 10% decline in house price will drag household real consumption growth by about 1.5%pt.

Table 1: Drivers for consumption growth
Dependent variable: Real consumption growth
<table><tr><td></td><td colspan="2">2003Q1-2009Q3</td><td colspan="2">2009Q4-2022Q4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Coef.</td><td>P-value</td><td>Coef.</td><td>P-value</td></tr><tr><td>Constant</td><td>0.46</td><td>0.82</td><td>0.18</td><td>0.84</td></tr><tr><td>Income</td><td>0.30</td><td>0.05</td><td>0.86</td><td>0.00</td></tr><tr><td>House price</td><td>0.15</td><td>0.01</td><td>0.16</td><td>0.01</td></tr><tr><td>Equity price</td><td>0.00</td><td>0.53</td><td>0.02</td><td>0.10</td></tr><tr><td>Interest rate</td><td>-0.49</td><td>0.03</td><td>-0.09</td><td>0.78</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan. All equations include lagged values of dependent variables.

By way of contrast, equity price movements do not have a significant impact on household consumption. An interesting change is that, while interest rate increases used to have significant negative impact in data until 2018 (link), that impact becomes insignificant as the sample data extends to 2022. This may be because the household leveraging process ended (household debt as % of GDP rose rapidly from 17% in 2008 to 62% in 2020, then stabilized in recent years) and households no longer have an incentive to further lever up when interest rates move lower. An alternative explanation, based on the permanent income hypothesis, suggests that the impact of interest rate movements on consumption can be ambiguous. Lower interest rates reduce the value of assets but increase the present value of future income. The fact that consumption has become less sensitive to interest rates might have more to do with the relative importance of net assets vs future income.

## Subsidies and transfers: temporary lift

Many argue that fiscal policy should now take a leading role in supporting stronger consumption growth, supplemented by monetary easing. But there is debate over the form that support should take.

The government’s preference, as demonstrated in practice, is for trade-in subsidies for durable goods, such as autos and home appliances. The logic is that this will benefit both the demand and supply sides. Nonetheless, the scale of fiscal support has been modest, and more importantly, the policy effect depends on the capability to front-load future demand. Once such measures have been taken, the room for further frontloading of demand may diminish. A recent example is that, despite the recent step-up in fiscal subsidies for autos, auto sales have remained soft, declining 6.2%oya in June and 4.9% in July (link).

Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

A popular proposal is for transfers to the household sector, via cash, digital payment or consumption vouchers, a policy practice that has scaled up after the Pandemic outbreak in 2020 (link). However, the effect of a cash transfer policy will likely be temporary, and the window of opportunity (e.g. during lockdown in 2022 or immediately after reopening) appears to be behind us. One simple issue is that any universal cash/consumption voucher handout implies a big fiscal burden (given a 1.4 billion population). This is part of the reason why the government is reluctant to do so, while a more targeted cash/ consumption voucher can get trapped in the political debate around the selection criteria of beneficiary households. As a result, we think the likelihood of this policy option being adopted remains low.

## Boosting income expectations is critical

The above measures are not sufficient to address the primary issue: how to boost household income. Disposable income is the primary driver of consumption. China’s consumer confidence index collapsed after 2022 lockdowns, and has not recovered despite economic reopening in early 2023. Weak consumer confidence came along with weak income and employment expectations, the latter falling to a new record low recently (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Consumer confidence
![](images/f00922368a79fcb035d4568432ae4ed8b3bad825115dc920585dd40412188fae.jpg)

Weak income expectations are associated with a mismatch in China’s economic transformation. There is strong policy support for manufacturing upgrade and new economic sectors. But these coincide with regulatory headwinds in the old economy (housing) and some service sectors (e.g. education, internet, health, and financial activity). Despite the rapid growth in new economy sectors and strong growth in manufacturing investment, these sectors are capital-intensive and do not generate many new jobs. Meanwhile, average wage growth is soft amid intensified competition and disinflation in goods prices.

The service sector used to be the primary source of employment growth over the past decade, but this was not the case in recent years. While the COVID lockdown has passed, the impact of regulatory headwinds has persisted with job losses and pay-cuts in affected sectors. This was amplified by compensation cuts on the official side (due to fiscal difficulties for local governments), and compensation caps and anti-corruption measures in the financial sector and the SOE sector. Reported cases of compensation clawback have only affected a very small group of people in certain sectors, but resulting worries about income uncertainty have damaged sentiment among upper-income groups.

In the first seven months of the year, individual income tax fell 5.7%oya, compared to +5.4%oya household income growth in 1H24 as reported by the NBS (Figure 7). The discrepancy can either arise from reporting errors in household income data, or it may indicate a distributional problem that a negative income shock concentrated among upper-middle income families who pay tax (the number of individual income taxpayers is only around 100 million in China, 7% of the total population). The biggest threats for China’s consumption recovery are weak income expectations, the erosion of consumer confidence, the shrinking of upper-middle income groups and the new trend of consumption downgrade.

Figure 7: Household income
![](images/d72ff552d222004aabd12d2df98fbd991280e5b21e7fd2762fb4285719149005.jpg)

To boost consumption, in our view, policy measures need to be taken to ensure income growth is faster than GDP growth. That means policy should be redirected toward more balanced support across sectors. In particular, that means more benign and predictable policy in the service sectors, seeking to revive potential innovation, bring back jobs and restore market confidence. In the manufacturing sector, containing excessive competition and protection of workers’ privileges would be helpful to support the labor share in the output.

## Reducing the household saving rate

Another direction is lowering the household saving rate, as advocated by the IMF recently (link). While the high saving rate is related to weak income expectations or income uncertainty as discussed above, the government can also take measures in two areas to reduce household saving rate: to reduce inequality, and to improve social safety networks.

Tingting Ge (852) 2800-0143 tingting.ge@jpmorgan.com Ji Yan (852) 2800-7673 ji.yan@jpmorgan.com

A reduction in income and wealth inequality can be helpful to support consumption, as the propensity for consumption is higher for low-income than high-income households. This explains the policy focus on common prosperity in recent years. But if the aim is to support consumption, common prosperity should not mean compensation caps in high-income sectors, clawback of remuneration, or forced donations. Even if these steps can be justified by redistributive goals, it should be recognised that they create pervasive uncertainty which undermines consumption.

Improving social safety networks is another channel to reduce precautionary saving. China’s social security spending (excluding employment-related social security expenditure) started from very low levels (2.4% of GDP in 2007), steadily improved between 2010 and 2018 and rose to 7.1% of GDP in 2018, but since then has remained within the range of 7.0-7.5% of GDP (Figure 8). The government has expanded the scope of the social safety network to cover the rural population, but the level of coverage remains low, with notable tiered differences between different groups (government employees vs urban citizens vs rural citizens). Hence, there is room to improve the social safety network, especially for disadvantaged groups.

As we pointed out earlier (link), to reduce income and wealth inequality, there are various policy options at different stages. At the primary distribution stage, measures can be taken to reduce income gaps between urban and rural areas (new urbanization strategy) and across regions (regional coordinative development plans). At the re-distribution stage, tax reforms can help, e.g. introduction of new taxes on capital and wealth (e.g. property tax, capital gain tax and inheritance tax), fiscal transfer to support low-income areas or disadvantaged groups. At the third distribution stage, incentive schemes (e.g tax deductability for donations) can be established to encourage donation on a voluntary basis.

Figure 8: Social security expenditure
![](images/9a0bcdc492769f1a161b1119b78f28e091e036300eb29d2034597ed2de299dca.jpg)

Source: IMF, MOF, J.P. Morgan

Compared to cash transfers, improvement of social safety networks can be considered a broader and more lasting form of fiscal transfer. Similarly, fiscal transfer can be in the form of fiscal support for public housing, public services or publicservice related investment. Fiscal policy can be calibrated to other social objectives alongside consumption support: examples include subsidies to encourage young people to get married and give birth, and to support elderly-care business.

Taken together, the government can redirect policy support for inclusive growth and improvement of social safety networks. These measures may not have an immediate policy impact, as the policy lags can be long and impact modest in the short run, but making such policy changes are important to achieve balanced and sustainable growth in the long run.

## Housing stabilization measures matter

In addition, stabilizing the housing market is also important. An extended correction has been a major drag on economic growth and a source of financial stability concerns (e.g. local government hidden debt and regional banks). Consumption has been dragged by negative wealth effects and weak housing-related expenditure (e.g. furniture, home decoration). Policy measures to stabilize housing have been seen since 2022, but that adjustment has been gradual and piecemeal.

Housing activity worsened again in August (link), so we may see further policy steps soon. These often involve relaxation of rules on second home mortgages, easing home purchase restrictions in non-core tier-1 cities (HPR has been eliminated in non tier-1 cities), or further relaxation of sales restrictions. But, in our view, the government needs to take more forceful action. This could include creating a large-scale real estate stability fund to ensure home completions and to support normal funding demand by developers. The central government and/or the central bank could also step up the support for government purchase of housing inventory and conversion of public or rental housing (see here and here).

The challenge in the housing market is a negative spiral between weak home sales (which has entered an overshooting stage), expectations for further home price decline (as house price correction is incomplete) and high home inventory (reinforced by weak home sales). One solution is to let home prices fall quickly, so that home price expectations will stabilize and home demand will come back, and housing inventory will start to fall. However, while house price declines can help improve affordability, they would damage the household sector’s balance sheet as home ownership rate is high (80- 90% based on different estimates), cause social instability or trigger financial distress. Hence, the government needs to be careful. In our view, large-scale support from the central government or the central bank to accelerate efforts for local governments to buy housing inventory and convert into public housing is a more desirable policy choice in the current situation, which can stabilize the housing market with smaller side effects on the economy.

## United States

0 At the September FOMC meeting, we expect the Fed will do what it “should” do and cut 50bp...

… warranted by a shift in the balance of risks and a desire to preclude any further labor market weakening

• Although CPI surprised more firmly in August, we are tracking just a 0.14% gain in core PCE last month

Next week’s retail sales, IP data should firm, suggesting upside risk to 1.5% 3Q GDP growth

Fed officials have made it quite clear that an easing cycle will commence at the upcoming September FOMC meeting next Wednesday. The main question the markets are grappling with is how large the opening move will be. Following the firmer-than-expected CPI release on Wednesday, market pricing moved decisively toward 25bp, but a pair of news reports suggesting it would be a close call moved the market to price around a 50% chance of a 50bp move next week. We agree it is likely to be a close call, but we also believe the Fed will make the “right” move and go 50bp.

Arguments for just a 25bp ease include the possibility of panicking the market with a larger cut and the importance of reaching consensus in the first “consequential” move—especially right before the election. We don’t find the first argument compelling: Powell has proven to be an adept communicator and should be able to convey that a level shift is intended to reinforce their forecast for a soft landing. Plus, the market’s repricing should minimize any risk of panic from a larger move. We are somewhat more sympathetic to the second argument, as we think there is a strong desire to reach a consensus next week—although we take the Fed at its word that the election is not a consideration.

That said, the case for a 50bp cut seems clear to us: various iterations of a Taylor Rule imply policy is currently a full percentage point or more too restrictive; the shift in the balance of risks suggests front-loading rate cuts via a risk management approach; the fact that “several” Fed officials were willing to begin cutting in July supports catching up in September after two disappointing job reports.

## Go big to eventually go home

The case for the Fed to front-load rate cuts this year is clear in our view after the pivot in the risk assessment from Chair Powell at Jackson Hole, the July FOMC minutes, and even Governor Waller last week (“the balance of risks is now weighted more to the downside” on the employment objective). So too is the case for getting back to something close to neutral much sooner than the leisurely pace put forth in the June Summary of Economic Projections, which had the policy rate still above 4% at the end of next year and above 3% at the end of 2026. Assuming the Fed does indeed announce a 50bp reduction in the funds rate target, we expect the median 2024 dot to show a total of 100bp of cuts this year, and for 150bp of easing in 2025 (Figure 1). We also expect Powell to argue this action should help preserve a soft landing (even if he has avoided using that exact phrase).

Table 1: Median June SEP Projections and our Forecast for September

Source: Federal Reserve, J.P. Morgan

Conversely, should the Committee take the timid approach and end up cutting only 25bp next week, we would look for the median 2024 dot to show just 75bp of cuts this year. In that case, we would expect Powell to stress a readiness to promptly pick up the pace on any signs of further labor market softening. Indeed, he is likely to have to parry a phalanx of dovish questions from reporters. In either case, we look for relatively modest changes to the economic projections.

We look for the statement to say the Committee has gained further confidence on inflation moving sustainably toward 2%, after citing “further progress” (dropping the preceding “some” from prior iterations). Waller’s recent remarks could provide a template for updated guidance: “With inflation and employment nearer our longer-run goals and the labor market moderating, it is likely that a series of reductions will be appropriate.” Any such guidance would, of course, be couched within data dependent language. We don’t expect any change in balance sheet policy, although the rate reduction could be characterized as normalizing policy akin to the continuation of QT. The minutes for next week’s meeting could indicate that the end of balance sheet reduction is on the horizon.

## Ebbing inflation worries

This week’s comprehensive set of inflation measures collectively suggested a soft reading for core PCE inflation in August, and continue to point to dissipating inflation worries despite slightly firmer than expected CPI and PPI reports. Earlier in the week, the August CPI inflation report showed a core CPI increase of 0.28%m/m, the largest gain since April, which kept the year-ago rate stable at 3.2%. The headline CPI rose 0.2%m/m, marking the smallest annual increase since

early 2021 (2.5%). However, the three-month run rate for core CPI was a reasonable 1.8% annualized pace in August (Figure 1). The primary driver of the underlying firmness in the CPI print was shelter, and excluding shelter the core rose 0.08%. While rent inflation moderated slightly, other shelter categories firmed. Core services increased 0.4%m/m, while core commodities fell 0.2%m/m.

Figure 1: Core CPI run rates
![](images/cb6018c4a172250dd1010469ee07960ec0329a04305fa01539d948075bcb6520.jpg)

Following the CPI report, the August PPI release also showed firmer-than-expected readings. Headline PPI rose 0.2%, and core PPI (excluding food and energy) increased 0.3%, bringing the year-ago figures to 1.7% and 2.4%, respectively. Despite these upward surprises, the import price index data for August were softer than expected, with the headline falling by 0.3%. This softness was mainly due to imported fuel prices, and prices excluding fuel edged down 0.1%. The yearago pace remained at 1.3%, the fastest annual inflation rate since December 2022.

Considering all the inflation data received this week, we expect core PCE inflation to settle at 0.14% in August. If realized, this would inch the year-ago pace up to 2.7% from 2.6% in July. Much of this move is due to base effects, as the three-month annualized run rate would be just 1.85% if our tracking estimate is accurate. The overall easing of inflation concerns—particularly in the Fed’s preferred PCE measure— is one factor that reinforces our conviction that a 50bp rate cut next week is appropriate.

## Jobless claims still doing fine

Amid an array of economic data that has mostly pointed to slowing growth in employment and rising unemployment, the data on jobless claims continue to look quite good. Weekly initial claims are hovering around 230k, which is roughly unchanged from the same time last year. Seasonally adjusted claims were elevated last summer and then took an especially large drop in mid-September, after which claims remained mostly under 220k until May of this year. Residual seasonality may have boosted seasonally adjusted claims in the summer and we would not be surprised to see claims take another step down through the rest of this month.

Continuing jobless claims are also sending a less worrying message than they were at the start of the summer. Continuing claims took a clear step up from late May to late June, but since have stayed in a narrow range. That said, one worrying item of note is that the exhaustion rate—the ratio of claimants drawing their final payment (after exhausting eligibility) to first payments six months’ prior—kept steadily rising through July, the latest month for which we have data (Figure 2). The 12-month average of the exhaustion rate is now at late 2015 levels. This appears at odds with the unemployment data, which shows only a very small change in the mean and median weeks of unemployment, perhaps pointing to a shift in the mix of people who are using unemployment insurance.

Figure 2: Claims exhaustion rate vs. duration of unemployment
![](images/349c31d14f9ac5ed095282a6806874d6958e30e5c2c7a8260526b8cb019832c5.jpg)

## Next week’s data should be mostly decent

Next week brings some key activity data that will help firm up current-quarter GDP tracking, including retail sales, industrial production, and housing starts—along with a first look at September business surveys. The auto sector was likely a drag on retail sales and a support to IP in August, contributing to a forecasted 0.2%m/m fall in overall retail sales but supporting a 0.7%m/m gain in IP as production schedules show a rebound in factory output following July shutdowns. Stripping out the motor vehicle sector we expect a decent 0.3%m/ m increase in retail sales, and also a 0.3%m/m rise in manufacturing output. If our forecasts are realized they could lend some upside risk to our 1.5% forecast for 3Q growth.

Hurricane Beryl was also likely a drag on some July indicators, which will support an August rebound. In addition to IP, where the Fed estimated weather held back both manufacturing and mining, housing starts also likely declined in response to the hurricane. Single-family starts had plunged in July, with a large decrease in the south, and while starts were probably set to decline in response to elevated inventories some of the unusual drop could be weather-related.

We expect a soft reading of -4.0 on the Empire manufacturing index for September, little changed from August and a weak reading though one that has improved from notably worse prints earlier in the year. Broadly the different manufacturing surveys have been on the soft side in recent months, with the PMI and ISM composites both recently giving up small gains and returning near their cycle lows.

The employment index in this report has been mostly very weak since the start of 2023 and in August it remained near the low end of its recent range. This mirrors trends in other Fed manufacturing surveys, which for the most part have showed steadily declining employment measures over the last couple years. It will thus be important to see if the employment measures in the regional surveys continue to move lower, or if they can at least stabilize.

Total ex, gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food sery.</td></tr></table>

Retail sales probably fell 0.2%m/m in August, dragged down by lower vehicle sales. Excluding autos we expect a 0.3%m m increase, and also a 0.3% increase in the control category (excludes food services, autos, gas, and building material stores). This forecast poses some upside risk to our 3Q real consumption forecast of 3.0%q/q saar, as if realized and absent revisions to past months, consumer spending for the quarter could quite easily hit 3.5%. Gasoline prices, which are down 30 cents/gallon in the last two months, will provide a tailwind to real spending through the quarter, though the current rapid pace of consumption is unlikely to last with job growth slowing and saving rates now back near cycle lows.

Already reported data on auto sales shows that unit light vehicle transactions declined 4.5%m/m in August, which will drag down overall retail sales. Turning to the other major categories, Chase card spending data suggests that control retail sales will continue to grow a decent amount in August fol-

lowing robust gains in earlier months that have resulted in the category rising close to 5% on a 3m/3m saar basis. Card spending data and rising employment at restaurants also point to another gain in food services sales. For building material stores we forecast a slowdown after two very strong months. While building material store sales have improved this year, a trend mirrored by improvements spending in the construction spending report, employment levels at these stores have declined for three straight months.

<table><tr><td>Tue Sep 17 9:15am</td><td>Industrial production %m/m,sa, unless noted</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>Industrial production</td><td>May</td><td>Jun 0.3</td><td>Aug -0.6</td></tr><tr><td>Manufacturing</td><td>0.8 0.8</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.7 0.7</td></tr><tr><td>Motor vehicles and parts</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.3 0.3 -7.8</td><td>6.0</td></tr><tr><td>High-tech</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Mfg ex motor vehicles</td><td>1.1 0.9</td><td>-0.9 0.0</td><td>1.0 0.3 0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Business equipment</td><td>0.3</td><td>-0.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Capacity utilization (%,sa)</td><td>78.3</td><td>78.4</td><td>-0.2 78.3</td></tr><tr><td>Manufacturing</td><td>77.6</td><td>77.5</td><td>77.8 77.2 77.6</td></tr></table>

Industrial production likely recorded a strong 0.%m/m gain in August as some of the factors that led to a 0.6%m/m fall in July are reversed. July saw an 8% plunge in motor vehicle production as summer auto plant shutdowns were unusually large, but production schedules indicate that this was reversed in August. The Federal Reserve also estimated that Hurricane Beryl reduced manufacturing output by 0.3%, and that there was likewise a drag on mining as the weather stopped some natural gas extraction. There was no mention of an impact on utilities output, though the large 3.7% fall in July was considerably greater than we had expected based on electrical utility output data from the Edison Electrical Institute, so Beryl may have also had an effect there.

With the large gain in auto production we expect that total manufacturing will increase 0.8%m/m, and 0.3% excluding autos. Employment in manufacturing was actually down 24k in August (-18k ex autos), which is sizable for that sector, but aggregate hours worked for production and nonsupervisory employees in the non-auto sector actually rose 0.2%m/m, which underpins our forecast for continued growth here. If realized, manufacturing output ex autos would be up at a \~3.5%q/q saar pace in 3Q, the best rate since the end of 2021. This segment has generally been rising since the start of the year, partially reversing a decline that began at the start of 2022, with about two-thirds of that gain coming from chemicals and petroleum (chemicals include pharma). However, to put things in perspective, it is worth noting that total manufacturing output has hardly changed in the last decade (ex COVID).

<table><tr><td rowspan="5">Tue Sep 17 10:00am</td><td colspan="5">Homebuilders survey</td></tr><tr><td>Sa</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Aug</td><td rowspan="4">Sep 41</td></tr><tr><td>Housing market</td><td>43</td><td>41</td><td>39</td></tr><tr><td>Present sales</td><td>48</td><td>46</td><td>44</td></tr><tr><td>Prospective buyer traffic</td><td>28</td><td>27</td><td>25</td></tr></table>

We look for the NAHB survey to improve slightly in September, rising to 41 from 39. The index has fallen steadily from its recent high of 51 in March/April though it remains within the range of the past couple years. The weakness in the index vs. the pre-COVID years, when it was routinely over 60, is at odds with the actual pace of new home sales, which have been fairly steady over the last year and are similar to prepandemic. The large post-pandemic decline is linked to the run-up in mortgage rates, and while declines in mortgage rates in the last year haven’t yet boosted the index we do expect it to eventually improve.

<table><tr><td>Wed Sep 18 8:30am</td><td>Housing starts Million units, saar</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>Starts</td><td>May 1.32</td><td>1.33</td><td>Aug 1.24</td></tr><tr><td>Single-family starts</td><td>0.99</td><td>0.85</td><td>1.32 0.95</td></tr><tr><td>Multifamily starts</td><td>0.32</td><td>0.99 0.34 0.39</td><td>0.37</td></tr><tr><td>Permits</td><td>1.40</td><td>1.45 1.41</td><td>1.43</td></tr></table>

We look for housing starts to rebound 6-7%m/m to a 1.32mn annualized pace in August, and for permits to increase close to 2% to 1.43mn. This increase in starts would reverse much of the large decline last month, which was led by single-family units. Single-family starts fell 14%, but permits were roughly flat. Starts are always more volatile than permits, so divergence within a month is not unusual, but the July gap appears quite large. Most of the decline at the national level can be attributed to the South which had an especially large fall. This could reflect temporary weakness related to Hurricane Beryl, which is another reason to think that starts will at least partially rebound.

While we do expect some recovery in single-family starts for August, builders will probably maintain lower levels of starts than they did during the recent highs in late 2023 and early 2024. This is because the inventory of unsold homes is elevated and builders may prefer to let these run down first. Lower mortgage rates could help this occur via higher sales as opposed to lower starts. However, new home sales have

not gotten particularly weak to begin with, likely supported in part by homebuilder incentives such as mortgage rate buydowns. Consequently, lower rates may not provide as much of a boost to new home sales as would normally be the case.

<table><tr><td</table>

We expect that initial jobless claims fell to 225k in the week ending September 14, down from 230k the prior week. This is the payroll survey week, and if the forecast is realized claims would be down 8k from the August week and the four-week average would be down 6k. Unlike some other labor indicators, jobless claims continue to send a fairly optimistic message, as they are essentially unchanged from the same time last year.

It is possible that some residual seasonality boosted claims during the summer and that like last year around this time seasonally adjusted claims could take a step down. In the same week last year claims fell 16k, and while that may have been partly volatility following the Labor Day holiday, claims subsequently stayed under 220k most of the time until May of this year. Consequently if claims remain around the 230k range in coming weeks it would be consistent with a slight deterioration in conditions relative to last year.

Continuing claims have also been constructive recently. After taking a step up between late May and late June they have since fluctuated in a narrow range, and are down a bit from their late July high when claims were still being boosted by Hurricane Beryl and auto plant shutdowns.

We think the Philadelphia Fed general business activity index will edge up a bit to -5.0 in September from -7.0 in August. This survey has looked better than many of the other regional

Fed surveys, though it was especially strong in July (13.9), and the plunge to -7.0 in August can be read as a pullback from what was likely an unsustainably positive level. However, the weighted composite was still a healthy 52.7 in August, with new orders at 14.6 and shipments at 8.5. Consequently, the fall in the general business activity index may have overstated weakness in August.

One area where this index had been worse than other surveys was employment, though that changed in July when the index jumped to 15.2, well above any of the other regional surveys. It’s fall back to -5.7 in August put it squarely back in the middle of the pack, though values closer to -10 would be more consistent with where it spent much of 1H24.

<table><tr><td rowspan="2">Thu Sep 19</td><td colspan="5">Existing home sales</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Aug</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="5">10:00am</td><td>Total (mn, saar)</td><td>4.11</td><td>3.90</td><td>3.95</td><td>3.85</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-5.1</td><td>1.3</td><td>-2.5</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-13.2</td><td>4.6</td><td>4.5</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply (nsa)</td><td>3.7</td><td>4.1</td><td>4.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>5.2</td><td>4.1</td><td>4.2</td><td></td></tr></table>

We forecast that existing home sales declined 2.5%m/m in August to a 3.85mn saar pace. The NAR pending home sales index, which leads existing home sales by 1-2 months, declined to a new record low in July, and we expect existing home sales to likewise edge down and match the cycle low that was set in October of last year.

Mortgage rates have continued to steadily fall and are now down around 75bp in the last three months. This is likely to support sales later this year, but there still hasn’t been much signal of this. For example, mortgage purchase applications, which also lead existing home sales, were trending down in July and August and as of early September were only marginally above recent lows.

On the inventory side, the months’ supply has now returned to pre-COVID levels, but the absolute level of inventories remains very low, down 30% from five years ago. Even with lower mortgage rates many potential sellers are sitting on significant mortgage rate lock in. The most recent rates quoted by Freddie Mac are at 6.20%, whereas roughly four-fifths of fixed-rate mortgages have a rate below 5%. Still, as the magnitude of mortgage rate lock-in is reduced the number of units available for sale should increase.

CPI inflation was slightly firmer than expected in August, as the core increased 0.28%m/m, which left the y/y rate stable at 3.2%, though based on this report as well as the PPI and import prices figures our estimate for the core PCE deflator is a smaller 0.14%m/m. The headline CPI increased 0.2%m/m (2.5%oya), in line with expectations, and the smallest %oya increase since early 2021.

We had expected shelter price inflation to moderate in August, and while that was largely realized for rent (0.37%m/ m) the OER (0.50%m/m) and lodging away from home (1.8%m/m) categories actually firmed. The margin between OER and rent was one of the largest in recent decades, although that came after a sizable--but less unusual--undershoot the prior month. We think this is temporary and that based on more mild inflation in new rent measures the rent and OER segments will continue to moderate in coming months. Excluding all of shelter the core rose 0.08%m/m, and excluding just OER and rent it increased 0.14%. Shelter gets a smaller weight in the PCE than the CPI, which is one reason we expect that the PCE will be up less than the CPI. Focusing just on core services, the total was up 0.4%m/m, ex shelter 0.2%, and ex OER and rent 0.3%, with all of those being the largest gains since April. In contrast core commodities fell 0.2%m/m, similar to recent months.

Away from the shelter measures, there was sizable 3.9%m/m increase in airline fares, the most in over two years, whereas medical care services surprised to the downside again, falling 0.1% after a 0.3% decline the prior month, though these prices are still up 3.2%oya. Turning to goods, apparel (0.3%m/ m), new vehicles (0.0%), and used vehicles (-1.0%) all had higher rates of change than in July, though none of these are particularly firm. Industry data suggest the ongoing decline in used vehicle prices could at least temporarily reverse in coming months. Medical care commodities meanwhile fell 0.2%, the first drop since January.

Outside of core, food prices rose 0.1%m/m while energy declined 0.8%, with both of these just a touch softer than our expectations. Food price inflation, likely one of the more visible metrics for a typical household, has posted moderate gains over the last year that are similar to the pre-COVID pace, following the big run-up in prices in 2021-2022.

## PPI (Sep 12)
