Following the firmer-than-expected August CPI report, final demand producer prices for the month of August also were a bit firmer than we or consensus had anticipated. Headline PPI rose 0.2% on the month while core PPI (ex food and energy) rose 0.3%. However, unlike the CPI, both of these PPI measures were revised lower in July. The net effect was to deliver nearly as-expected year-ago inflation rates for headline PPI of 1.7% (down from a revised 2.1%oya for July), while the yearago core inflation rate firmed a tenth as expected, but came in at 2.4%oya as July was revised down a tenth to 2.3%. Similarly, the 3-month annualized rate for core PPI ticked up to 2.0% from 1.9%, but has cooled recently relative to much more elevated readings earlier this year.

In the details, a -0.9% drop in energy prices helped to bring the headline PPI number down relative to the core measure. Food prices were nearly unchanged (up 0.1%), with a large jump in crude food prices offset by a fall in processed food prices. Outside of food and energy, producer prices for core goods rose 0.2% on the month, while producer prices for services were up 0.4%, although a more modest 0.3% when trade services (a measure of firms’ margins) are excluded. The BLS flagged a 4.8% jump in the price index for guestroom rentals as a major factor in the August rise in final demand services prices. Among the components that impact core PCE tracking, we estimate a small positive contribution from airfares and medical care, once those indexes are seasonally adjusted..

<table><tr><td colspan="2">%m/m, nsa, unless noted</td><td rowspan="2"></td><td rowspan="2"></td><td colspan="3"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Jun 0.0</td><td>Jul 0.1</td><td>Aug 00</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Import prices</td><td></td><td>0.1</td><td></td><td>1.7</td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>1.5</td><td>1.6</td><td>1.6</td><td></td><td>1</td><td>0.8 -0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Ex-fuel import prices</td><td>0.2</td><td></td><td>0.1</td><td></td><td>0.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>10</td><td>1.1</td><td>12</td><td>1.3</td><td>15</td><td>1.3</td></tr></table>

After upward surprises in the August CPI and PPI reports this week, the import price index data were mostly a touch softer than expected for last month. The headline import price index fell -0.3%, taking the year-ago pace down to a five-month low of 0.8% from 1.6%oya in July. A significant part of the downward movement in August was due to imported fuels prices; imported petroleum prices alone fell -3.2% last month. Excluding fuels, import prices edged down -0.1% in August—although the year-ago pace remained at 1.3%, its fastest annual inflation rate since December 2022. Based on the trio of inflation reports from this week (CPI, PPI, and import prices), we see the core PCE price index tracking a 0.14% rise in August, with the year-ago pace inching up to 2.7% from 2.6% in July. Much of that gain represents base effects, however: the three-month annualized run rate would be just 1.85% if our tracking estimate is realized.

In the details, imported auto prices were unchanged on the month, while the year-ago pace of price growth has been relatively elevated at 2.3%oya. That likely reflects an apparent continued shortfall of US auto inventories, even as they have been largely catching up to pre-pandemic levels (outside of a modest reversal over the past month or two). Imported consumer goods excluding autos fell -0.1% on the month and have been trending around 0.5%oya for much of this year, after jumping out of deflationary territory at the start of 2024. Imported capital goods prices, in contrast, were up 0.1% on the month but are running at just a 0.3%oya pace over the past twelve months. They have escaped disinflation more recently, and it seems likely that non-fuel import prices generally should firm moderately on a forward basis given the lagged nature of the pass-through of USD moves into import prices, all else equal. Although the trade-weighted US dollar has strengthened modestly over the past several weeks, the broader trend has been a depreciation—of around 2% since midyear.

Consumer sentiment in the preliminary September University of Michigan survey edged up to 69.0 from 67.9 in August, with current conditions up 1.6pts and expectations up 0.9pts. Sentiment is still down around 10 points from the recent

March high, although likely 6-7 points of that came from the switch to web surveys from phone surveys and when looked at over a long horizon it appears that sentiment could be continuing its gradual trend of improvement from the cycle low in mid-2022. Lower gas prices, falling interest rates, and strong asset prices may further boost sentiment in the coming months, though the absolute level of sentiment today is still low.

Inflation expectations in this report continue to be quite benign, as one-year expectations fell to 2.7%, a cycle low, and similar to the pre-COVID period. Five-year expectations did increase to 3.1% from 3.0%, which is on the higher-end of the post-COVID range, though these are still fluctuating within a narrow band.

This month was the first rise in current conditions since March. With the election season in swing, there continues to be some notable recent divergences among sentiment by political party affiliation. Sentiment among Democrats has been mostly flat between May and September, except for a dip after the Biden-Trump debate, whereas Republican sentiment has declined steadily since March. Note the preliminary survey finished before the recent Harris-Trump debate.

13 September 2024

# Focus: Recent immigration and unemployment rates

Last week we touched on the effect of immigration on employment growth, in particular the effect from unusually high growth in an “other” immigrant group which includes immigrants who are on humanitarian parole, who are awaiting an aslyum hearing, or who are otherwise in the country illegally. A related question is how this immigration is contributing to recent changes in the unemployment rate. We estimate likely at most a couple tenths of a percent.

The first condition for the other immigrant group to influence the unemployment rate is that they must be captured in the household (CPS) survey. The CPS survey frame is constructed by sampling from addresses and then physically visiting them for an initial interview, so it does not depend on immigrants having registered with any government agency or having a telephone or utility customer account. Consequently, there is a reasonable chance that these immigrants are captured in the survey, though there is difficulty in capturing migrants housed in emergency shelters (for example in August there were over 60k migrants in NYC shelters).

Even if immigrants are captured in the survey, population controls could still bias their final weight in the sample. The CPS uses geographic, age, sex, race, and Hispanic/Latino ethnicity controls, with estimates of the total population in each bucket based on lagged data on births, deaths, and migration. There is no control for foreign-born status, so if the foreignborn share increases they will correctly get more weight in the CPS. However, immigrants are correlated with factors such as race and Latino ethnicity, so their total weight could still be biased, since the population controls for those groups are not responding in real time to immigration changes.

Figure 1: Change in share of the labor force that is foreign born %pt change over 12 months
![](images/b44b541e9a9859447eced922e5c02e03452179d68f5089f4b3a72ae013e5185a.jpg)

With that in mind, the foreign-born labor force share in the CPS has risen about 0.5%-pt faster per year since the start of 2022 than the 2010-2019 average (Figure 1), which at the size of today’s labor force would work out to about 75k/month for the labor force and 70k/month for household or payroll employment. To re-iterate, this calculation is based on looking at changes in the share of foreign-born workers, not the published levels, since the growth in levels is widely thought to have missed some recent immigration. In their recent population projections, the CBO concluded that net immigration from legal permanent residents and nonimmigrant visa-holders would likely remain near past trends, so it seems fair to infer that much of this acceleration reflects the other immigrant category. That is possibly still understating the foreign share. For example, the Brookings Institution estimated that in the total working-age population, the foreign-born share as of January 2024 in the CPS was about 0.4%-pt too low.

However, our objective here is just to estimate how much this extra immigration has added to the as-reported data. Consequently, we need the product of two quantities: the share of the labor force that is attributable to the faster-than-normal growth in other immigration, and how different their unemployment rate is from the rest of the population.

Figure 2: Native vs. foreign-born unemployment rates Percent, 12mma
![](images/80d62a4f8cb9c1875e595fd64111181f3c84677b520eaec1920e54bb1b902aed.jpg)

Since the start of 2022, had the foreign-born share of the labor force grown at its 2010-2019 average, than the share would be 1.4%-pt lower. Since there may have been some “catch-up” immigration from regular legal channels after COVID this is likely an upper bound for the share attributable to excess immigration in the “other” category. To raise the unemployment rate even 0.1%-pt they would need to have an unemployment rate 7% higher than the total population.

In the last year, the unemployment rate for foreign-born workers has been just 0.1-%pt higher than for native born (Figure 2). However, recent arrivals who sometimes lack work authorization will have higher rates. The CPS microdata provides a flag to isolate immigrants who have arrived from 2022 onwards. Unemployment rates for this group in 2024 are 5%-pt higher than the total foreign-born population, and 6%-pt higher for those with at most high-school education. Even assuming the “other” group is higher still, their total effect on national unemployment is likely at most a couple tenths of a percent.

## Euro area

• ECB cuts rates by 25bp

• Staff forecasts and Lagarde’s comments signal a continued quarterly pace of cuts

A sharp drop in the PMI could reignite a debate ahead of the October meeting, but that is not our baseline

• Euro area IP still weak in July

This week, the ECB cut its deposit rate by 25bp to 3.5%, as expected, and also narrowed the corridor in line with the changes it announced a few months ago to its operational framework. This narrowing is not, however, significant from a macro-economic perspective and more about future-proofing the system for the time when excess liquidity is much lower. That point though is still a few years away.

Most importantly, the policy statement, press conference and new staff forecasts all signalled continuity, which suggests that the ECB is currently minded to continue with quarterly cuts of 25bp. The door to an earlier move in October is open as the ECB stuck to its data dependent and meeting-by-meeting approach and gave no pre-commitment on rates, but, in our view, a significant disappointment is needed for an October cut to become a real possibility. As that meeting is only five weeks from now, we suspect that, absent an external shock, only a relatively sharp drop in the PMI could bring October into play. The PMI’s decline would, however, have to be large and go well beyond any Olympics-related payback in France.

As we do not expect a large drop in the PMI, we remain comfortable with our call of the next 25bp cut being in December. We do expect an eventual acceleration in the pace of cuts, but only after the ECB has gained more confidence in the disinflation journey, and have pencilled this in for after March. We also continue to see the terminal rate at 2%. In our view, this is consistent with the staff projections as they show inflation returning to target by late 2025 or early 2026 and with rates declining to 2%, in line with the market curve in mid-August.

Changes in the staff forecasts were almost exactly in line with our expectations. Growth was revised down for the near term and seems to now sit below a 1% annualised pace through year-end, but still shows an eventual pick-up to around a 1.5%ar (Figure 1). The ECB has been surprised by the composition of the recent growth data, with consumers still failing to respond to the rise in real incomes. But, as a baseline forecast, a response by consumers has simply been delayed. Business investment has also been a source of downside surprise, but it is also expected to improve, helped by lower rates. Given the PMI’s relatively low level (excluding the French Olympics effect), a large further decline could raise significant concern, but, some post-Olympics payback in the PMI has likely been factored in as the 2H24 growth forecast has been cut significantly. Hence, the PMI would likely need to drop quite sharply beyond any Olympics effect to trigger a rapid reassessment by the ECB ahead of October.

Figure 1: ECB staff forecast for real GDP %q/q saar
![](images/8504070150bf47e7a581a25bf14306858bf11193a460982597d979212784a942.jpg)

The discussion and forecast for inflation also showed continuity in a way that points to gradual cuts, rather than faster ones. The core inflation forecast was increased quite significantly for 2024 and 2025, as we had expected, reflecting recent upside surprises, especially on services inflation (Figure 2). In fact, the forecast for 2025 may have been close to a 0.2%-pt upward revision to 2.4%oya, rather than to just 2.3%oya, but, the forecast still moves down to 2% by the spring of 2026.

Figure 2: Euro area core inflation
![](images/4d070bebc79fbe916c67a5ecb2a078685b97767f0ba075bb06c6fb8fe25801c5.jpg)

This narrative is underpinned by the way the staff is reading the data on wages, which acknowledges strength at present and still expects a slowing next year (Figure 3). And that disinflationary impulse will be reinforced by a pick-up in productivity to around 1%, even if this has been delayed (Figure 4). In addition, profit margins are expected to make a modest contribution to inflation. Hence, while the stickiness of services inflation remains a challenge, the broader news on the GDP deflator, wages and profits is on the right path (Figure 5). In fact, the GDP deflator came in below the ECB’s latest forecast in 2Q24. Taken together, the progress is still subject to uncertainty and therefore keeps the ECB on a gradual cut ting path, at least absent a sharper deterioration in growth.

Figure 3: Euro area compensation per employee
![](images/3b4ede86c1fdd6fe088f4598d4529377c178a82a80e47d57ab3c2d596b360eb1.jpg)

Figure 4: Euro area labour productivity
![](images/9a9a6a8e052ed16417317b3adedbe1d8b4871e0defdb8d14ce6d1f93c1450ffa.jpg)
Figure 5: Euro area GDP deflator

![](images/9c8d7883de065991f5b009aeb9eb27263fff1275247ae4b8e2a448d73d6a8b54.jpg)

## Another IP decline in July

Beyond the ECB decision, there were limited data released this week. News on the manufacturing front continue to be disappointing. Euro area IP ex. construction declined 0.3%m/ m sa in July, with manufacturing output down 1.1%m/m. This left the July IP level 2.5% ar below the 2Q level, with manufacturing output 5.8% ar below 3Q.

Ireland remains volatile (the Irish data are impacted by patents and the owning of production facilities outside the country). Irish IP jumped 9.2%m/m in July, with manufacturing output down 1.3%m/m. Excluding Ireland, the July Euro area

IP decline thus looks significantly larger at -1.2%m/m (manufacturing output ex. Ireland is broadly in line with the total Euro area aggregate).

Euro area IP overall has been on a downward trend for the last two years. There is no sign of improvement in the short term based on business surveys (Figure 6). The August manufacturing output PMI remains depressed at 45.7, with demand and sentiment indicators also weak.

Figure 6: Euro area manufacturing output and PMI
![](images/ead41d14ec6c9d70f03604df834436a0ed04a17ec391a0ace1d6d152ff6c5c77.jpg)

Looking at the IP industry details, output across energy-intensive companies remains very weak despite the lower level of gas prices (compared to the 2022 peak). Car production meanwhile remains on a downward trend. The country details furthermore show German IP being very weak compared to its pre-pandemic level.

Figure 7: Euro area IP ex. construction
![](images/bdd14cae3c9cb871ef30f539bf4e3b89a11a958f621f2b833f123977c791edb6.jpg)

The core flash reading, in our view, was affected by a temporary impact from the Paris Olympics, which boosted services prices (hotels and transport in particular). The details in that respect will be important to monitor. If our assumption is correct, services prices in an underlying sense were running at a 3.5% ar pace in August (i.e. excluding the Paris Olympic effect). This is less than the 3.5% ar pace seen in the early part of the year (linked to price re-setting and a VAT rebound in German restaurant prices), but is broadly in line with the pace seen on average during 2023. Services prices thus seem to have been sticky in an underlying sense of late.

## Review of past week’s data

## Japan

• BoJ to keep policy settings unchanged next week…

• ...but signal it remains committed to further unwind of policy easing

• Focus on Ueda’s assessment of inflation outlook as recent commentary leans hawkish

• Sentiment data suggest two-speed economy through 3Q

The BoJ will almost certainly keep policy settings unchanged next week. The Bank has consistently signaled a gradual approach to policy rate hikes, driven by rising confidence in achieving its inflation target. Given solid but not overheating fundamentals, as well as recent market volatility, the BoJ is likely to conclude that there is no rationale for an additional rate hike next week—even as it guides toward future rate rises.

This message of future policy rate hikes was reiterated this week, with Board Member Tamura joining the chorus of recent guidance. His speech emphasized the objective of hitting a neutral level of rates of at least around 1% this cycle. While some of this messaging appears to have been lost in translation, our read of the speech was hawkish. The consistent signaling from the BoJ over the past few weeks also looks in line with our long-standing view that the market is underpricing the Bank’s normalization path.

Away from policy, the incoming data suggest that the economy remains on track for an above-trend 2% or so expansion this quarter. Sentiment data were mixed this week, as we had expected, with the services sector continuing to suggest resilience as domestic demand firms. Manufacturers, by contrast, appear to be somewhat more downbeat, with unfavorable weather and sluggish China-related demand depressing activity through September. This divergence is broadly in line with our judgment of a two-speed economy, with growth increasingly driven by domestic demand rather than a function of the external cycle.

## BoJ: The start of a virtuous cycle

The focus will be on the BoJ's assessment of the recent favorable domestic data and the conditions for the next rate hike. Economic data through 3Q have pointed to a continued recovery in private consumption, supported by rising wages from favorable spring wage negotiations. While recent yen appreciation has reduced some upside risk to inflation, strong wage momentum and signs of domestic demand recovery are increasingly likely to push up underlying inflation through year-end. This suggests that the drivers of Japan's inflation are shifting from exogenous cost factors to to an endogenous virtuous cycle. This dynamic could inherently justify policy rate normalization for the central bank, so it will be noteworthy to see to what extent the BoJ acknowledges this.

Since the rate hike decision in July, concerns about a US slowdown have increased, causing instability in Japan's financial markets as well. However, the lesson the BoJ learned from the market volatility may not be the "risk of raising rates," but rather the "risk that could arise from delays in forming rate hike expectations." With a persistent domestic mindset that the low inflation and low interest rate environment will continue almost indefinitely, the BoJ's rate hike guidance after NIRP removal was not received in a balanced manner, with the market focusing mostly on dovish aspects. This resulted in accumulation of one-sided positions in the market, causing large volatility in August.

Given this lesson, the BoJ is likely to continue sending messages about the gradual scaling back of monetary policy accommodation as domestic economic fundamentals improve and likely will proceed with the next rate hike without delay. We continue to forecast that December will be the timing for this, as the trend of further wage increases and their reflection in the underlying inflation rate will be confirmed by then. In 2025, as the continuation of the virtuous cycle is confirmed, the BoJ likely will continue raising rates to 1% by the end of the year.

## A little less resilience in 2Q GDP revisions

Revisions to the second estimate of 2Q GDP were small, with the economy expanding 2.9%q/q saar, compared to 3.1% in the first estimate (Figure 1). This moderate downward revision was, however, unexpected. We and the consensus had projected GDP growth to look a little stronger in the second print, largely on the back of stronger-than-expected capex data in last week’s MoF corporate survey.

Figure 1: Japan Real GDP
![](images/c4d0a8dec372408d7ba7000c9872935f12d7e1304f863206c2655e20342656ea.jpg)

A slightly weaker profile for domestic demand was the source of the revisions. Capex growth was cut by half a point to a 3.1%q/q saar expansion. Private consumption was trimmed to

3.7% from 4.0%. And public investment was also revised down modestly. Overall, domestic final sales now look to have contributed 3.6%-pt to headline GDP growth from 3.9% previously. On the flip side, the drag from net trade looks a little smaller (0.1%-pt vs. 0.2% initially) given an upward revision to export growth.

The modest revisions do not change the fundamental picture of activity holding up into 3Q, with the recent monthly data suggesting that firm wages, profits, and sentiment will translate into resilient consumer spending and corporate investment this quarter.

## Small-firm sentiment rises...

In the August Economy Watchers Survey, sentiment among small and medium enterprises (SMEs) was stronger than our forecast, rising 1.5pt to 49.0. This marks the fourth consecutive monthly increase, indicating a substantial recovery in activity. The household-related index showed solid improvement, reflecting resilient demand supported by strong base wage growth. This positive trend is also evident in the optimistic outlook index, which rose 2pt to 50.3.

Looking at the details, the household-related sentiment on current conditions improved 1.8pt to 49.0, driven by the resilient consumption momentum. Although this partly reflects the seasonal effects of the summer vacation, robust growth in wages appears to bolster consumer confidence. While the business-related index fell 0.3pt to 48.4, the overall trend remains stable. The employment index rose 2.6pt to 49.7, implying solid labor demand. Each outlook sub-index rose about 1 to 2 pt (Figure 2). In the respondents’ comments, SMEs noted that they are gradually passing on higher input costs to output prices, contributing to their firm outlook, also supported by a recovery in demand.

Figure 2: Economy Watchers Index, 2-3 months ahead outlook
![](images/3ddf39c6d151a22be04b23d2577a1cbadd48cd5ece1d7657e06e35e9e26f41f5.jpg)

## ...even though corporate confidence slips

The September Reuters Tankan survey of large manufacturers’ sentiment fell 6 points to 4, marking the lowest level in 7 months, which was below our expectations (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Japan Reuters Tankan large firms sentiment - Headline DI, level of conditions, boxes denote 3-mo outlook
![](images/9035279e12e468674f29253ec095c12d2c1266b73b16a0acda34952880693674.jpg)
Source: Reuters, J.P. Morgan

While stormy weather may have contributed to this decline, the more significant factor appears to be sluggish demand from China, which has eroded the confidence of some large manufacturers. By contrast, sentiment among non-manufacturers remained relatively stable, dropping only 1 point to 23. Despite the typhoon, retail sector sentiment remained elevated, consistent with recent resilience in consumer spending and sentiment data (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Japan Reuters Tankan, retail DI, level of conditions
![](images/185c9e0d1b27638ccdb2fe9bb5bf4e91c1a482f9a06a2812ccb4206168bbc76a.jpg)

Some other sectors experienced sharp drops, possibly due to the effects of the typhoon. The food processing sector fell 17 points to 0, and the textile sector dropped 29 points to -9 . However, the food sector's outlook index remains high at 15, indicating that this decline may be a one-off event. On the flip side, sentiment among auto producers and electronics remained stable (auto: 0, electronics: -14) after last month’s drop (Figures 5 and 6). The relatively weak figures are likely related to ongoing weakness in demand from China, as indicated by survey respondents.

The manufacturers’ outlook index is only 1 point higher than current conditions, suggesting that weak sentiment may continue in the coming months. We will gain clearer insights with the release of the next September PMI in two weeks.

The Producer Price Index moderated to 2.5%oya in Aug, possibly reflecting the yen appreciation after the July BoJ meeting. The impact of the FX moves is also evident in soft export and import prices; contract currency based measures suggest that almost all of this decline is due to the stronger yen. We believe that the source of inflation pressure is gradually shifting from the exchange rates to endogenous factors such as higher wages and firmer domestic demand.

## Canada

Macklem spoke on monetary policy risks from global shift toward greater protectionism

• Further cooling in August CPI inflation keeps the BoC easing 25bp-per-meeting into next year

• A strong rebound in July retail sales should support better 3Q real goods PCE growth after 2Q drop

In a speech this week, BoC governor Macklem discussed the outlook for global trade. He noted that the pivot toward protectionism and industrial policy is a challenge for an open economy like Canada (where, he noted, about one-third of income is derived from exports). Specifically, slowing globalization could put more upward pressure on inflation, while trade disruptions could mean larger deviations from the BoC’s inflation target. While never mentioned explicitly, there is little doubt he has in mind risks of tariffs that could materialize after the US election in November. However, we think the combination of the tight integration of the two economies as well as existing trade agreements make Canada relatively insulated from such shocks. By contrast, its other USMCA partner—Mexico—faces a more delicate challenge to balance inbound Chinese investment with US politics.

## August CPI should cool further

Next week’s August CPI report is likely to support continued easing by the BoC. Specifically, we look for headline CPI to have eased to 2.1%oya in August—helped by downward pressure from energy prices—while core CPI ticked down to 2.6%oya last month. Lower prices for durable goods and a drop in prices at the pump should keep headline inflation on an easing trend in August. Goods inflation has softened to just above zero in year-ago terms, while continued strong growth in shelter prices has kept services inflation firm (Figure 1). While we expect some moderation in mortgage interest costs as the BoC proceeds with its easing cycle, shelter price growth will remain elevated in August.

Figure 1: Select categories of CPI
![](images/22b0df3a870142236924119efaad8c06e0767fa451e6c6c2a97441331bbf5319.jpg)

Meanwhile, core inflation should cool further. Our forecast implies a seventh straight month with the year-ago rate for exclusionary core (excluding food and energy) inside the target zone. Moreover, we see the year-ago rates for the BoC’s preferred core measures softening another tenth (to average 2.5%oya) in August. Such an outcome should reinforce the BoC’s current dovish lean. This easing in inflation, alongside concerns for further near-term softening in activity (particularly the labor market after last week’s disappointing report), has allowed the BoC to be among the leaders in DM central bank easing so far this year, with a third consecutive 25bp policy rate cut last week. We see a 25bp-per-meeting easing pace continuing at least through 1Q25 with the policy rate reaching 3% by midyear. That said, risks remain tilted toward a faster and deeper easing cycle.

## July retail sales to show rebound

Next week’s July retail sales report will provide a first look at the momentum in consumer goods spending at the start of the current quarter. We look for total nominal retail sales to bounce back 0.6% in July after declining in June. While we expect core retail sales—which exclude sales at gasoline stations and auto dealers—to tick up just 0.1% on the month, this modest July gain would come on the heels of a solid June rise. We forecast retail sales in volume terms to have advanced 0.5% in July.

Figure 2: Real retail sales and real PCE %3m/3m, saar (PCE is %q/q, saar)
![](images/df1dc9e4cf9e1dd3c2b32269d7904dc17261d8952afe2ebbab2e7ce0891caf4f.jpg)

While our July retail sales forecast is consistent with real goods consumption tracking a solid 2%ar gain in 3Q24—after falling 1%ar in 2Q24—we expect some moderation in coming months and for real consumer spending this quarter to average around 1.0%ar or so given the somewhat less favorable backdrop in terms of the labor market and household indebtedness (Figure 2). Meanwhile, last week’s July merchandise trade data point to a continued drag on growth. Our GDP nowcaster and the output-based GDP data from Statistics Canada both point to sub-1%ar growth in Canada this quarter, after a firmer-than-expected 2.1%ar outturn in 2Q. Given it is still early in the 3Q data calendar, we hold our current-quarter real GDP growth forecast at 1.0%ar.

## Mexico

• Inflation spun back to 5% last month driven by noncore dynamics and core goods

However, core services continued to challenge the disinflation process

Boosted by construction, industrial production perked up in July; manufacturing won’t take off

The ruling coalition achieved the supermajority in the Senate and kicked off the reforms season

Nothing changed notably on the macroeconomic front this week, with inflation confirming the correction in the recent non-core spikes and the underlying components more aligned with our view that demand-side pressures are easing. With the manufacturing sector struggling to take off, it was not surprising to see steady core goods prices—aided by receding pressures in raw food staples. The focus this week was notably on the institutional contours of the economy, with Congress moving quickly to approve the populist Judiciary reform.

Starting on the macro news agenda, August inflation allayed earlier fears of resurgence as the speed with which annual inflation spiked in June and July was more than matched by the pace at which it fell in August. Led by a meaningful drop in fresh fruit and vegetable prices, headline prices contracted in August and drove annual inflation from a local peak of 5.6%oya in 1H Jul to 4.8%oya in 2H Aug. The marginal headline CPI reading was lower than the consensus estimate by roughly a tenth in the second half of August.

Core was in line with our expectations at 0.10%2w/2w in the second half of the month and 0.22%m/m in August as a whole, driving core inflation to 4%oya, the upper bound of the target range, for the first time since 2021 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Headline and core CPI
![](images/629ef98dced381af6dc754e1b9bbb7ca8e8ff0d90b3e3720b26ef238279cbce3.jpg)

Core inflation was in line with our expectations, but the details were negative, pointing to underlying inflation pressures that had seemingly receded. On a sequential basis, core prices again rose 0.3%m/m, sa and 3.6%3m/3m annualized, close to their long-term norms. But this owed largely to a very benign print in nonfood goods prices, which were basically flat in the month. We’ve written how these may come under pressure (Figure 2) ahead on FX depreciation (even if cyclical considerations might temper it a bit).

Figure 2: Core and Non-core CPI
![](images/5851c6c556011562a91c30b953933289268b944ee83658e4f050ca984da6b503.jpg)

The concerning part is that prices of services excluding housing and education are accelerating again, particularly when excluding airfares and tourism packages. Non-tourism services price gains had persistently slowed through June but accelerated sharply in July and August and the %3m/3m annualized rate has moved back above 5%. We note that consumer spending has firmed recently, coinciding with the re-activation of government social transfers in July following their suspension during the electoral blackout period (March-June).

It will be important to track services prices ahead, as they have proved a sticking point for Banxico in the past. Still our less sanguine view on the economy suggests emerging pressures on services won’t be long lasting and should support inflation well below 4% by mid-2025.

## The manufacturing-construction divide

July IP was somewhat stronger than we expected, up a couple of tenths in the month, whereas we had looked for it to move a tick lower. The trend has improved after a very weak spell earlier in the year, when continued manufacturing sluggishness compounded with a lull in construction spending that dragged down overall industrial activity. To very varying degrees, both industries have turned the corner.

Construction has seen the most noticeable shift (Figure 3), averaging a 1.2%m/m gain in the five months through July (+2.6%) following similarly sized declines in the preceding four months. The sector is tracking another strong quarter in 3Q. This has come alongside renewed strength in government infrastructure spending, notably in the energy sector, both hydrocarbons and electricity. Transportation has not seen a similar trend, though. Public investment surged massively (+50%m/m, sa) in July.

Consumer confidence

Figure 3: Industrial production
![](images/3121c6e77ef41864ee236fbbcf25db7dc9e9719ab47ba881f42408e866d8b687.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan

The trend in manufacturing is in a different quadrant. The sector has been moving sideways for the past couple of years. July was weak on account of a more than 3% drop in transportation manufacturing, but this came after a 2% pop in June that left output up 2% in July vs. 2Q saar. We think the balance for manufacturing is for the first time in a while looking up as exports and production are becoming more broadbased. Non-auto manufacturing is still low but has grown in each of the past three months. The surge in intermediate imports, which move hand in hand with non-auto exports, also supports this view.

Still, despite this pocket of optimism, construction is on track to moderate significantly in the next quarters on the back of the post-electoral hangover in infrastructure, and manufacturing is expected to soften on non-encouraging expectations for the US industry.

While IP is tracking a 3% annualized gain early in 3Q, the July GDP data out in two weeks’ time will complete the picture with service sector activity. J.P. Morgan’s Nowcast is once again pointing to contraction in 3Q, challenging our short-term optimism for an upward revision. Needless to say, we continue to think that risks beyond this quarter are to the downside.

## Institutions shaken to the core

After 10 days of tense discussion in Congress and with the governing party Morena reaching the supermajority in the Senate near the eleventh hour, the Judiciary Reform has now been approved by more than 17 states—enough to ensure the Legislature can now turn over the approved document to the president for his signature and its publication in the Official Gazette.

All eyes will be on the rest of the reforms agenda, but we expect little to happen in the second half of September—or at least that can have a market impact similar to the Judiciary discussion. The remaining constitutional changes will be managed by the incoming government of President-elect Sheinbaum. For us, the main focus will be on the bills that

have budget impact: Energy and Social spending. The Morena coalition now has a de facto constitutional majority in the Senate (Figure 4) so we expect swifter actions in the rest of the year.

Figure 4: Upper House composition Senators

![](images/a2a1aff286e74b851fa8ddfc927cf2a3bb37c2a3afbe87bf0b83a9f4e4050f48.jpg)
Source: Analítica parlamentaria and J.P. Morgan. \*Ruling coalition including "grasshopper" senators

## Brazil

• BCB to meet following Fed’s decision on Wednesday

• Board set to hike after better marginal CPI numbers...

• ...but faced with upside risks amid heated economy

• Fiscal review to be another highlight of the next week

After starting an easing cycle in August 2023 and ending it after the May meeting, BCB is about to embark on a tightening cycle. We and consensus are aligned in expecting BCB to hike the policy rate by 25bp to 10.75%, in what we think will be a unanimous decision. In August we changed our call from stability to hikes assuming that this decision would reflect the board’s wish to accelerate inflation convergence towards the 3.0% target over the 18-month-ahead horizon. From where we stand today, we believe that this strategy will be chosen amid some mixed signals on the economic backdrop as well.

This week’s CPI revealed positive news, decreasing 0.02%m/ m, nsa in August and surprising both us and consensus by showing lower-than-expected core inflation after a spike in July. However some crosscurrent forces on inflation ahead do suggest that the upside risks are larger than the downside risks for our 4.2% forecast for the year. The droughts registered in the country are likely to pressure prices upward, especially food and electricity.

Figure 1: Headline and core CPI (IPCA)
![](images/b996f4c08d837e81bbd1031b1338b174bf9d7406ca63e83b3f65480f7507d7d3.jpg)
Source: IBGE and J.P. Morgan

On activity IBGE releases showed stronger-than-expected services and broad retail sales results. In both cases there are arguments to take the headline figures with a grain of salt. Services output grew above 1% in both June and July, a result that was likely distorted by professional and administrative services according to the National Statistics Agency. In retail, marginal expansion was just one-tenth above expectations for the core result. Even so, there is no getting around the fact that even with some payback on industrial production August data reinforce upside risks to our 2.0%q/q, saar forecast (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Brazil 3Q nowcaster
![](images/d5afd15a24da60a0f19b9a448294bfd101d5e61024376aeb89bebd2a1a2a769f.jpg)

Due to COPOM’s quiet period, BCB board members’ recent speeches all came before the release of the indicators mentioned above. Still, they signaled a smooth cycle beginning as some mentioned that an eventual tightening cycle would be “gradual”—a word that was associated with the 25bp pace in 2011 and 2016, for example.

We expect the communication to tilt hawkish, but it is unclear whether it will be sufficiently so to corroborate market expectations. We think BCB won’t give clear signals about the pace and extent of the overall cycle, leaving the door open for accelerating the pace, or not. One possibility is that BCB says something along the lines of “the board deems policy rate adjustments over a prolonged period as adequate to ensure the timely convergence of inflation to the target.”

What comes next will depend especially on the behavior of economic growth, inflation, inflation expectations, and the exchange rate. Considering some moderation in growth and the expected Fed easing, despite the more challenging inflation picture in 4Q, we see BCB raising the SELIC rate up to 11.50% by the beginning of next year. An important assumption that is not directly related to these factors, but does impact them, is the effects fiscal policy will have on activity and inflation over coming quarters (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Contribution of fiscal policy to GDP growth
![](images/d7df90fd9c5ecd9a0e6412d6d56a165296f27b7826758b40865d7438ad43192a.jpg)

In that sense, we are edging closer to another key date on the fiscal calendar: the release of the fourth bimonthly fiscal review, expected next Friday. Treasury Secretary Rogério Ceron recently suggested that the outlook for revenues changed enough that some unfreezing of expenses may be

warranted, but cautioned that the scenario remains challenging for expenses—which may lead to an increase in the number of blocked expenses (‘A eventual alta de juros não estará atrelada à deterioração fiscal. Os números provam isso’, diz secretário do Tesouro’,” Globo, September 10).

As was the case in July, we do not think the government will deliver results in line with official estimates, rather anticipating lower revenues and a target miss. While we assume that what matters in practical terms is the overall result between freezes and blocks, we do caution that even a tighter net adjustment may not appease market participants that grew concerned with recent signals from Brasília. Between all the headlines arising on that front, we single out three.

First is the proposal to change the cooking gas stipend paid to low-income families, increasing its reach from 5 to over 20 million families until 2026. The proposal entailed a change in how such expenses are accounted for, effectively creating an extra-limit subsidy that would substitute what is now a fully accounted expense. Second is speculation about potential measures to fight the negative impacts of the drought happening in some parts of the country, such as potential extraordinary stipends to fishermen. Finally, we have the decision to move forward with non-recurrent revenue measures to offset the payroll tax break. Faced with resistance from BCB, which is responsible for the central government financing needs estimate that is stacked up against the formal primary target, politicians approved an ordinary bill that does not force BCB to include the appropriation of all these measures in their calculus, but explicitly says that such resources may be considered by National Treasury for target purposes.

In all these cases, the volumes pale in comparison with the size of the federal budget and its challenges. Forgotten resources will apparently reinforce 2024 federal cashflow by less than BRL10bn (under 0.1% of GDP). Headlines regarding measures to combat the drought stand at less than a tenth of that from a fiscal perspective (‘Auxílio a pescadores do Norte deve ter impacto de R\$ 470 mi,' Poder360, September 12). Then cooking gas stipend auxílio gás seems set to increase around BRL10bn between 2024 and 2026 according to officials, but Finance Minister Fernando Haddad signalled that the accounting issue will be reviewed after general criticism.

Such developments are important not so much because of what they mean individually for 2024, but rather for what they signal for the fiscal framework future as we edge closer to the 2026 general elections. Market observers seem to assume that they are not good omens (‘Governo propõe driblar orçamento para aumentar auxílio-gás, que atingirá pico em ano eleitoral,' Globo, August 29). As much as we are willing to anticipate some marginal positive impact of efforts taken in the right direction, such as those geared toward reducing fraud and reviewing social security expenses, we agree with the bearish interpretation and believe that recent developments reinforce the negative tail of our balance of risks for fiscal accounts in the medium term.

## Argentina

The Lower House ratified the total veto of the pension bill

• Headline CPI increased 4.2%m/m in August (236.7%oya and 94.8% YTD)

Core CPI again proved sticky to the downside at 4.1%m/m, with core ex. food at 4.3%m/m

• Advance in the release of capital controls to ensure a sustainable disinflation path

News on the week was a mixed bag. The Milei administration saw good news on the political front, gathering the necessary votes in the Lower House to ratify the Presidential veto of the pension bill with the support of PRO and dialogist opposition parties. This allowed the president to protect the fiscal equilibrium, the main anchor of the economic program; the pension bill, if approved, would have implied a fiscal drag of 0.4% of GDP in the short term that could have escalated above 1% of GDP in the medium term. Moreover, Minister Caputo anticipated that in August the fiscal accounts would be balanced again, after the headline deficit reported in July on the back of the interest payments reported to bondholders.

Meanwhile, the financial markets also welcomed positive developments, as the tax amnesty inflows alongside some BCRA intervention narrowed the FX gap below 30%. That said, while the narrower FX gap and the increase in USD private sector deposits since mid-August (US\$1.6bn) helped to replenish gross reserves, net reserves accumulation remained challenged, likely requiring an IMF waiver for the 3Q target.

On the inflation front, the news was not as encouraging: headline CPI printed above expectations, and more importantly, core CPI proved sticky for the third consecutive month, highlighting the need for the current policy framework to evolve to secure a sustainable disinflation path ahead.

## Core inflation stickiness lingers

Headline CPI increased 4.2%m/m in August (236.7%oya and 94.8% YTD), a tad above our and market expectations of 3.9%. The monthly inflation print thus proved relatively stable around the 4% monthly level, on the back of regulated price adjustments and sticky core prices. Seasonally adjusted (by J.P. Morgan), national headline CPI gained 4.3%m/m, sa, slightly below the last three months’ average of 4.7%, leading the last-three-month sequential pace to slow slightly to 73.6%, saar.

As anticipated, core CPI proved again sticky to the downside at 4.1%m/m, in line with our expectations and above the prior three months’ average (3.7%m/m) (Figure 1). Similarly, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (adjusting core inflation by excluding food prices) printed at a sticky 4.3%m/m, compared to 4.2%m/m in the prior 3-month average. Worth noting, ex. food core momentum is running at 71% in annualized terms (sa), compared to 72.3% the previous month.

![](images/ef4ed9f4ec0e587c5542e5568d501728e74f8fe2ba1656fd5783a8cd1ad74b0e.jpg)

By component, food inflation also remained fairly stable on the month at 3.6%m/m (+1.1%-pt contribution), compared to the 3.7% average reported in the last three months. Fresh food (mainly vegetables) contributed 52bp, while non-fresh food’s contribution increased to 54bp (from 41bp the previous month).

As noted above, monthly headline inflation was again impacted by higher regulated price increases after the tariff adjustments in August, with their contribution increasing by 0.4%-pt from the prior month. Indeed, in August there were increases above headline inflation in communications (+4.9%m/m) due to telephone services tariff hikes; transportation (+5.1%m/m) due to hikes in gasoline prices and public transport; education (+6.6%m/m); and housing and basic services (+7.0%m/m), the latter on rents and electricity and gas tariffs. Meanwhile, seasonal prices strongly decelerated to 1.5%m/m (+0.2%-pt contribution), from 5.1%m/m (0.8%-pt) last month, offsetting inflation pressures (Figure 2).

Figure 2: National-CPI
![](images/fd8431d5eb89a394681709b06995214940741c887a96d2481cebb1ba41e31c5d.jpg)

Data releases and forecasts Week of September 16 - 20

## September inflation to ease

The reduction of the “PAIS tax” rate, which became effective September 2, lowers importers’ costs, aiming to reinforce the disinflation path ahead after inflation has proved sticky around 4%m/m since May. In our estimates, the monthly (one-off) impact on the CPI basket of the “PAIS tax” reduction will be 0.6%-0.7%-pt, helping to drive disinflation. Indeed, the high frequency data for the first week of September is tracking at 3.4%m/m, with weekly food CPI running at 0.4%w/w. That said, achieving monthly inflation rates below 3% amid the current capital controls web remains a challenge.

Against this backdrop, we see monthly inflation averaging 3.6%m/m through November, with a modest rebound by yearend/early 2025, assuming a transition into a new policy framework and FX market unification. This is consistent with the December 2024 inflation forecast at 127%oya. Amid a stabilization program and a sustainable FX regime that spurs a credibility shock, we then see room for inflation to decelerate to a 2% monthly level on average through 2025, consistent with year-end 2025 inflation at 35%oya (Figure 3). Admittedly, the risk is for the delay in the release of capital controls weighing on economic activity and the FX gap, and adding upside pressures on inflation as the October legislative elections cycle approaches. In all, we view continuing with the relative price adjustments and advancing in the release of capital controls as two necessary conditions to consolidate a sustainable disinflation path ahead.

Figure 3: Headline CPI
![](images/3c20b127ab9571b81606e8a90469edc0ed20a4b8a7bef0863c68d3fcb0f26b69.jpg)
Source: INDEC and J.P. Morgan

## Andeans

• Chile: Aug’24 CPI logged 0.25%m/m, in line with expectations

• The 2024 year-end headline CPI stays at 4.5%oya

• Colombia: August CPI surprised lower...

• ...yet we expect BanRep to hold the 50bp easing pace this month

## Chile: A largely uneventful inflation report

The August CPI report proved largely uneventful, prompting no revisions to our modal projections. In the details, headline CPI logged 0.25%m/m in the month, driving the last 12-m inflation (referential CBC splice) to 4.6%oya, from 4.4% in July (Figure 1). Moving to underlying inflation, ex. volatile CPI came in on the soft side, up by just 0.1% on the month, in line with our forecast, and annual rate climbed to 3.7%oya. But seasonally adjusted, core logged +0.3%m/m, sa, with the last 3-m annualized pace running at 3.6%.

Figure 1: Chile headline CPI
![](images/02763f805398d90b7f49273322c3f9f410922f264a1401a63e544c775f010453.jpg)

In all, the CPI report for August does not alter our forecast, though it adds a bit of downside risk to our year-end headline CPI projection (of about 4bp). We thus keep our Dec’24 forecast for headline stable at 4.5%oya, a level that aligns with the updated CBC central scenario (see note). For next year, we project headline inflation at 3.8%oya by Dec’25, 20bp above the CBC’s forecast.

As discussed last week, following the Board’s dovish shift we foresee two more 25bp policy rate cuts this year. That would be consistent with the policy rate reaching 5.0% by year-end. We also maintain 4.5% as the terminal rate, based on the premise that r\* is at the ceiling of the CBC’s estimated range (3.5%-4.5%, with the CBC resorting to the midpoint as point estimate).

## Colombia: Food deflation in August

The CPI index stayed flat in Aug’24, coming well below expectations. Consequently, annual inflation continued its downward trend, reaching 6.1%oya, the lowest level since late 2021. Seasonally adjusted, the 3-month annualized pace decelerated to 4.3%, from 6.5% in July. While still above the target range ceiling, once seasonally adjusted the gap has narrowed substantially (Figure 2). Regarding underlying inflation, core CPI as tracked by BanRep (ex. food & regulated) also gained 0.20%m/m, with the 12-month basis decelerating to 5.5%oya, down by 30bp with respect to July. The seasonal adjustment shows the last 3m annualized pace running at 4.4%.

Figure 2: Colombia headline CPI % , sa by J.P. Morgan
![](images/3fd298de975dd1e85cf55c67311c4482b5e0c7bbb81192af12fc685ea8d91470.jpg)

While the downward August inflation result bodes well for stepping up the easing pace, the disorderly manner in which diesel prices were adjusted higher makes us think BanRep will continue with a 50bp cut this month. Diesel prices, heavily subsidized in prior years, have little direct impact on the CPI basket, but material indirect impact via transportation costs. After months of indecision, the administration dictated a COP1,904 increase of diesel prices effective September, also announcing two similar nominal hikes for next year. This triggered an aggressive response from truckers, resulting in generalized strikes and road blockages, causing four days of severe disruptions in the distribution of fresh food items and gasoline. An agreement was finally reached and diesel prices were set only COP400 higher, with an equal correction set for December 1. Additionally, the two COP2,000 adjustments planned for next year were, for now, suspended. We expect next year’s adjustments will prove lower than initially planned, with the corresponding fiscal impact for 2026.

In summary, we expect the impact of diesel prices to be more moderate than we anticipated a few days ago, yet the transportation distortions may affect September CPI, particularly food prices. While temporary, these distortions may prompt cautious behavior from the BanRep Board at the end of this month, despite the favorable August CPI print. The majority may prefer to wait for the September CPI report before accel-

Policy rate

erating the easing pace. Thus, we tweak our monetary policy call for this month and now expect a 50bp cut (though note it is a close call if the Fed starts its easing cycle with a 50bp cut). We maintain as base case the Board cutting the policy rate by 75bp in both October and December, driving the policy rate to 8.75% by year-end.

In terms of CPI forecasts, we maintain our forecast of headline inflation converging to 5.4%oya by year-end, as August’s downside surprise offsets the estimated impact of diesel prices for the remainder of the year, though we acknowledge a bit of upside risk associated with the early September trucker strikes. For next year, our baseline is less optimistic than the BanRep’s, as we foresee headline inflation converging to 3.8%oya by year-end (versus 3.0% in BanRep’s central scenario), though we signal downside risk if the diesel adjustment is postponed or kept unchanged at December’24 levels.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## United Kingdom

• Wage growth remains firm but is nevertheless slowing

• Softer GDP removes upside 3Q risk, but housing market and jobs market look solid

Watch for persistence weighted services in next week’s CPI, not Taylor Swift

BoE on hold next week, but good case for a step up in the QT pace

The data this week showed a small downside surprise on GDP and some further slowing in wage growth, albeit to a still solid pace. We remain of the view that the BoE will proceed with quarterly rate cuts, with the next in November and low odds of a move at next week’s meeting. But we would agree that inflation risks are moderating, and that the main question to ask on monetary policy is whether the BoE could pivot to a somewhat faster pace of cuts. There is already a majority on the MPC with this bias, but most remain cautious for the time being.

While there may be some Taylor Swift noise in next week’s CPI report, we’ll be looking to see whether the BoE’s “persistence weighted” services metric produces another softer-butnot-weak outturn of 0.3%m/m. If it does then this would embolden the argument of the MPC’s doves. But there are still some inflation risks in the opposite direction from potentially better growth in the months ahead.

Nowithstanding this week’s downside GDP surprise, the RICS survey showed a clear step up in new buyer interest and expected sales in August (Figure 1), while job growth appears to be holding in. The revival in housing market momentum is indicative of growing household confidence against the backdrop of falling rates and solid real income growth. There is scope for consumption to do better than in our official forecast.

Figure 1: RICS sales balances
![](images/c87aa62cac3bd6672eb5bbe5b4db6dcd8cb721b79c4bfa4e1bc87e440a210b1c.jpg)

The more interesting part of next week’s MPC meeting will be the decision on QT. As we discuss more below, we see a good chance the BoE will surprise with an accelerated pace of gilt reduction.

## More noise than signal in the labour market

The ILO unemployment rate dropped another tenth to 4.1% in the three months to July, further unwinding increases seen earlier in the year. The usual caveats apply that there has been more noise in the data for a while now due to know sampling problems, and its not clear that there is a particularly trend in any direction at the moment. Likewise, the LFS household showed very strong employment growth of 265k over the same period, but the employment rate at 60.2% is essentially just back to where it was at the start of the year.

There was a decline in the PAYE payroll jobs data of 59k (Figure 2), with a downgrade to the prior two months which leaves three consecutive months of decline. As such, the three month change is -1% annualized. This is clearly weak on the surface, although caution is also warranted here as these data are very revision prone. We have previously seen similar declines that have been revised away. While yesterday’s REC jobs report was weak, other surveys (PMI, BCC, DMP) suggest that job growth is soft but positive overall and we still think this is probably the fairest characterization of the data at present.

Figure 2: PAYE payroll employment and LFS household survey employees
![](images/86514f3ad0aeb419e66b14b366ebc04f63519cab5133cb1dcd49acb884ea668c.jpg)

Meanwhile, vacancies continue to fall and the vacancy rate is back at pre-pandemic levels. Pay growth remains relatively high in comparison, however. The headline AWE pay data surprised a little on the downside in July, slowing from a revised 4.6% to 4.0% (%oya, 3mma). The underlying pay data came in close to expectations, however, with the non-bonus AWE slowing from 5.4% to 5.1%. The move in the private sector was similar, and on a high frequency basis this translated into a gain of 0.3%m/m. This was a touch softer than we expected, but was mostly offset by an upward revision to June. Overall, private sector pay is running at a 4% annualized pace compared to three months ago, and is on track to meet the BoE’s 4.8% year ago forecast for 3Q (%oya, 3mma).

This rate of pay growth remains still strong, and the latest alternative data from PAYE sources show ongoing strength into August. Nevertheless, the AWE measure has trended notably lower over the past year and is no longer challenging the BoE’s forecasts. That should give the BoE confidence to cut further, although with GDP growth running firm at present there is less pressure to go quickly. We expect the BoE to continue with a cautious quarterly easing pace.

## GDP disappoints in July

Monthly GDP in July (Figure 3) came in flat (JPMorgan 0.1; Consensus 0.2) for a second consecutive month and was hence a disappointment compared to the solid growth pace seen for most of 1H24. There were notable declines in manufacturing and construction of 1.0% and 0.4% respectively, while services output was up 0.1%. Services have been the main driver of growth in recent months, and continue to expand.

Figure 3: Monthly GDP
![](images/defadc2ec6cac71f10f1e221d70bf0703cc1d37b952085f531ba60f3c8144dd8.jpg)
Source: ONS

Consumer facing services rose 0.3%, and while the 3m/3m trend remains weak (0.4% annualized) we continue to think there is scope for a clearer step up. Other services registered a small 0.1% gain although was pegged back by a large 1.3% decline in professional and scientific services. The sectors outside of services can swing around. Transport and machinery manufacturing took a hit in July, and both IP and construction output in July appear to be showing payback from prior strength. This is consistent with the point made by the BoE that the 2.3% annualized increase seen in 2Q GDP probably wasn’t sustainable. Nevertheless, we wouldn’t take the July GDP outturn as indicative of the trend. The PMI remains upbeat and confidence within the private sector has been encouraging. We assume a rebound in activity in August. Nevertheless, the upside risk around our 1.5% annualized GDP forecast for 3Q (BoE: 1.6%) has faded, with the current data tracking a little light relative to that.

## Preview of next week’s MPC meeting

We look for the BoE to keep rates on hold at next week’s meeting. The MPC cut rates at its last meeting in a tight 5-4 vote. This reflected a belief that the general inflation outlook was improving, aided by several survey based and forward looking indicators that we recently discussed here. But with the underlying price and wage data still strong, the MPC was hesitant and at the time expressed the intention to ease cautiously in the near term. This point was again made clear in Bailey’s Jackson hole speech in which he said “we need to be cautious because the job is not completed”.

In our view there has been a slightly more dovish shift from the BoE under the surface in recent weeks. Its forecasts highlight the possibility of faster cuts if it were to gain confidence that the upside risks to inflation are fading. Its therefore possible that the BoE could pivot if an accumulation of evidence builds. But the required data surprises have generally remained elusive to date, and we therefore maintain our view that the BoE will ease at a quarterly pace for the foreseeable future.

While growth surprised modestly to the downside this week, underlying growth still looks ok. The jobs market is holding in and there renewed signs of a spurt in housing activity in the RICS survey this week. There is evidence that labour market pressures have continued to ease, as evidenced by falling vacancies at least. Inflation is close to target but is soon set to rise again owing to a spike in regulated energy bills.

We won’t get August inflation until next week, the day before the BoE meeting. The July data did bring a downside surprise, although largely due to some of the more volatile services components (airfares, accommodation). There was a hint of underlying softness in the BoE’s persistence weighted services measure, which we estimate rose by 0.3%m/m, sa. But more of the same would be required to signal a weaker trend. The PMI’s pricing component hints in this direction, which is why we mention above that the BoE is shifting more dovish under the surface. But the more trusted data aren’t delivering yet.

Our best guess is that the vote next week will be an 8-1 (with risks of a 7-2). We assume Dhingra (and possibly Ramsden) will vote to lower rates again. But there will be two camps not in favour of easing: those in the centre who may have given some consideration to doing so (Breeden, Bailey, Lombarelli) and those who have generally been more cautious (Mann, Greene, Pill). We don’t know much about incoming member Taylor, although we have previously speculated that he may lean in a more dovish direction. We’d put him in the same camp as Bailey for the time being.

We expect the BoE’s statement guidance to remain broadly unchanged, which was already quite vague and leaves many options open. The BoE will announce next week its QT pace for the forthcoming four quarters, commencing from October. After some speculation it could slow the pace from £100bn, the consensus appears to have shifted to expect a continuation at the current pace with speculation they could speed up. The BoE has given little away in advance of the decision, but we still think there is a good argument for the pace to increase. Our call is for an increase to £120bn (which we discussed in more detail here and here).

We look for another impact on accommodation prices in August from the second round of Taylor Swift concerts (Table 1). This pushes up the CPI by 0.1%-pts, comparable to the impact we estimated after the June data. The difference this time is that the concerts were clustered in London rather than around the country. On the one hand this could mean there is more concentrated price pressures, but equally it could be argued that the impact is smaller as there is more capacity in the capital. The choice of sampling date also matters, and a choice by the ONS to pick the earlier Tuesday in the month could mitigate some of the impact (Table 1). Our forecast would mean year ago services inflation comes in at 5.7%, only slightly below the BoE’s 5.8% forecast. The BoE has tended to exclude volatile components from its inflation assessment, and our forecast would be consistent with a 0.3-0.4%m/m, sa gain in the BoE’s “persistence weighted” measure of services inflation. That would be a slower pace than seen in the first five months of the year, and more in line with the last two releases, but still on the firm side overall.

## Emerging Europe

• CEE food inflation turns stronger in August

Russia: Inflation elevated at 9.05%oya, the CBR hiked 100bp

Inflation printed higher than expected in Romania, Russia and the Czech Republic in August, while Hungary surprised to the downside. Headline inflation in Romania printed at 5.1%oya (JPM: 4.9%), 2.2% oya in the Czech Republic (JPM: 2.0 %oya) and 9.1% oya in Russia (JPM: 8.9%). Hungary came in at 3.4% oya (JPM: 3.5%).

Food inflation (Figure 1) explains the deviation in both Romania and the Czech Republic. For Romania, unfavorable base effects and sequential acceleration pushed oya rates sharply higher. For the Czech Republic food inflation remained in negative territory, but significantly less than previously. The food shock is concentrated in vegetables and fruit, likely reflective of a poor domestic harvest. If weather and a poor harvest are indeed the explanation, this implies the next few months should continue to see food inflation above the normal seasonal pattern and food remaining as an inflationary force.

Figure 1: CEE: August food inflation
![](images/70b1abcbbfd2466f9867be19ee6af435eded0ecc443498601aaf9a1bb223e3de.jpg)
Note: Hungary average excludes August 2022

This poses a risk to the disinflationary dynamics within the region as food inflation has been below headline inflation and contributed to disinflation thus far. Czechia is particularly exposed to such a reversal. As Hungary and Russia have not seen similar dynamics, it might be too early to draw strong conclusions, but we are monitoring developments. For Hungary, food inflation was 2.4% oya down from 2.7% and in Russia 10.2% up from 10.1% in July (Table 1).

<table><tr><td></td><td colspan="4">%oya</td><td colspan="4">% m/m</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>May-24</td><td>Jun-24</td><td>Jul-24</td><td>Aug-24</td><td>May-24</td><td>Jun-24</td><td>Jul-24</td><td>Aug-24</td></tr><tr><td>RUS</td><td>9.7</td><td>10.4</td><td>10.1</td><td>10.2</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.3</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>HUN</td><td>1.0</td><td>1.1</td><td>2.7</td><td>2.4</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>CZE</td><td>4.4</td><td>-4.8</td><td>-3.8</td><td>-2.3</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.5</td></tr><tr><td>ROM</td><td>5.1</td><td>4.9</td><td>5.4</td><td>5.1</td><td>0.0</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.5</td></tr></table>

Implications for central banks are limited, but a combination of sticky core inflation and upside risks from food is likely to add to the prudent approach. In Romania, the August print represents an upside surprise in both core and headline for the NBR. Considering that the bank did not cut earlier this year because of inflation surprises, a similar rationale should be applied now. We think the NBR will not cut for the rest of the year, however the risk of one 25bp cut remains. In Russia, increased momentum in food comes on heels of already elevated core inflation and may strengthen the position of hawks at the CBR, raising odds of another rate hike by the board.

## Russia: Inflation elevated at 9.05%oya

Consumer price inflation remained little changed at 9.05%oya in August, surprising to upside (JPMe 8.9, consensus 9.0). Relative to our expectations, food was the main source of surprise (Figure 2). Within food, volatile fruits and vegetables, as well as milk and dairy prices were mostly to blame for stronger dynamics in food. It is not entirely clear if the pick-up in the fruits and vegetables was due to simply shifted seasonality or lower crops — central and southern Russia had experienced a cold snap earlier this spring. Beyond these items, our sense is that the underlying momentum in the food segment should remain relatively contained given the stability in global agricultural prices, unless RUB depreciates significantly.

Figure 2: Russia food inflation %m/m saar (J.P. Morgan estimate)
![](images/562f921d1ba570dff8564150d6494dd82615ed67bf959da7046ed42a3ded12ee.jpg)

Figure 3: Momentum in Russia's headline CPI and core inflation %m/m saar (J.P. Morgan estimate)
![](images/629cb207221e5a1d88c241fc57e50ee4ee5181cdd49675ec232399d2995f7794.jpg)

Our measure of core inflation — CPI ex. food, energy and regulated prices — remained about unchanged at 7.9%oya. Seasonally adjusted, momentum in core also stayed little changed at estimated 6.6%m/m, saar, with momentum in core goods easing to 4.5% ar and in core services strengthening to 11.0% ar (Figure 3). As growth in unit labor costs remains in the low teens, we suspect the pressures in services will last for some time.

We currently forecast inflation to end this year at 7.6%oya, and drop to around 4.6% by end-25, staying slightly above the 4% target (Figure 4). The aftershocks of 2022-23 fiscal expansion will probably continue to reverberate through the system in coming quarters, yet we anticipate growth to downshift considerably in coming quarters, with utilization of capital and labor coming off the highs. This should lead to eventual reduction of underlying inflationary (and labor market) pressures, bringing the inflation rate closer to target.

Figure 4: Russia's CPI inflation
![](images/83511a68dd0693cfc4df3b99727600d0b170d505e5c89c60687fda99c7fcc775.jpg)

## CBR Hikes to 19%

The CBR remained in a hawkish mode, hiking the policy rate 100bp to 19.0% (JPMe and consensus: 18.0) and delivering a pretty hawkish message. The statement’s outlook sentence assumes a very high probability of further rate hikes at the next meeting, while the interpretation of the recent data flow had a clear hawkish bias. The assessment of inflation risks worsened, with deteriorating terms of trade (hence concerns about RUB) adding to the woes; the governor made it clear that inflation forecast for end-24 will be revised up significantly from current 6.5-7.0%oya (JPMe 7.6). The recent slowdown in growth was attributed to tighter supply-side constraints rather than slower demand growth, with elevated inflation taken as the main signal around supply-demand developments. The CBR also remained unhappy about the lack of passthrough of higher rates into credit activity — loan growth, especially in corporate segment, has remained robust — and about extreme tightness of the labor market, even as the wage growth apparently began slowing from mid-year.

The post-meeting conference suggested that the board did not seriously consider to hold rate at this past meeting, with 100bp and 200bp being the main options. Coupled with other communications, this points to very high odds of another hike at the next meeting, which will be accompanied by revisions in key macro forecasts. The big picture of the CBR’s policy dilemma, in our view, has been whether to generate a gradual downshift in growth and inflation, allowing more time for the fiscal impulse of 2022-23 to be absorbed by the system and tolerating inflation somewhat above target in 2025, or to generate a more rapid cooling of economy, potentially a recession, reducing odds of another year of missing the target in 2025. CBR’s body language suggests that the second option has been preferred.

We changed our forecasts and now expect another 100bp hike to 20.0% at October meeting. We anticipate a partial reversal of recent tightening next year, with the first cut likely in spring, and rate reaching 13% by end-2025 (vs. 12% previously). With the CBR in hawkish mode, we suspect risks to our 2025 GDP forecast of 1.2% and our end-25 CPI forecast of 4.6%oya are be skewed to the downside, risks to the rate profile skewed to the upside.

We expect the CBRT to keep its policy rate on hold at 50% this week. The economic activity is slowing rapidly, but monthly inflation is likely to be above 2% in September due to the education, transportation, auto, tobacco price hikes despite declines in motor fuel prices. We expect the CBRT to kick off easing cycle with 250bp cut in November following benign October CPI print.

## South Africa

• SARB will likely cut the policy rate by 25bp to 8%...

• ...as inflation risks are now more balanced

• Two-pot pension reform adds some uncertainty to macro outlook

• We expect a neutral policy rate of 7.25% by 2Q25

We expect the SARB to kick off a relatively shallow easing cycle on 19 September, cutting the repo rate 25bp to 8%. Already at its meeting in July, two of the six MPC members considered a reduction in the policy rate. However, upside risks to the inflation outlook prompted the SARB to retain a cautious approach. We think that now inflation risks have shifted to be broadly balanced, even as the implementation of domestic pension reforms adds some uncertainty to the growth and fiscal outlook in the near-term.

In our view the SARB’s updated forecasts could show inflation easing to just 3.7% next quarter (SARB: 4.3%; JPMe: 3.6%), notably undershooting its 4.5% target. A drop in gasoline prices at the pump, softer food inflation and subdued core goods inflation readings are the main drivers of the improved outlook, with the USD/ZAR rand input assumption probably also revised down to 18.10 (from 18.35) (Figure 1). This should lower the inflation forecast to 4.7% (SARB: 4.9%; JPMe: 4.6%) in 2024 and 4.3% (SARB: 4.4%) in 2025. While inflation risks are better balanced in the near-term, the SARB may stress elevated uncertainty around the global outlook and the rand. The SARB’s risk assessment around the mediumterm inflation outlook therefore remains unclear, adding to reluctance to front-load easing.

Figure 1: Headline, services and core goods inflation
![](images/47d8962e879cebba60a37450a305c6b6bf7688759bd0e796a33ad73100b33bc9.jpg)

The recent implementation of the two-pot pension reform adds some uncertainty to the outlook. Since the last MPC meeting, the SARB released a report outlining estimates on the potential macroeconomic impact of the access to the savings part of the pension pot, which was previously not possible (except for particular circumstances, such as a resignation). According to the SARB’s modelling, the pension fund reform could add 0.1%-0.3%pt to GDP growth in 2024 and 0.3%-0.7%pt in 2025, with the lower end of the range the more likely moderate scenario and the upper end of the range the high withdrawal scenario. Other macro variables would also be impacted, with inflation 0.1%-0.3%pt higher in 2025 and the repo rate 20bp-60bp higher than in the absence of the pension reform.

It is currently unclear to what extent the MPC will incorporate the impact of the pension reform into its baseline projections, but it is likely to cite upside risks to growth and downside risk to the current account due to the reform. We have incorporated an impact on the lower end of the SARB’s estimates and believe the SARB will proceed with reducing the policy rate to a broadly neutral stances of 7.25% by the middle of 2025.
