## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia’s business and consumer surveys continue to lose steam

• Australia’s unemployment rate is forecast to hold at 4.2%, alongside an employment change of +20K

• NZ’s economy likely contracted 0.1%q/q in the June quarter

There was little in the way of data in antipodean economies this past week with Australia’s consumer and business confidence surveys filling the void. The relationship between the Westpac consumer confidence survey and domestic activity is far from robust, though we note the most recent decline (-0.5%m/m) further highlights pessimism across the household sector with the index meaningfully below the neutral 100-level (current reading: 84.6, Figure 1). The NAB business survey was also soft with both confidence and conditions losing steam. The readings are less alarming than the consumer-level data, but nonetheless suggest economic growth remains soft and unemployment biased higher.

Figure 1: Business and consumer confidence
![](images/a4865ff18f638c0e59d7ee978325e2a8d13ac50c241e80e9f72026088364a62b.jpg)

Our baseline is for real GDP growth to gradually improve from current levels as the combination of supportive household factors, in particular tax cuts from July 1, and increasingly positive real wage growth facilitate a recovery in household consumption. RBA policy is expected to remain unchanged and at restrictive settings until early 2025, though a more pronounced deceleration in inflation pressures has the potential to prompt more front-loaded easing.

Aside from inflation, labor market performance will also be an important determinant for the RBA. We look for stability in the August data, with the unemployment and participation rates to hold at 4.2% and 67.1%, respectively. Population growth remains strong at 3%oya and has lifted the breakeven level of employment to \~40K (the number of jobs required to hold the u-rate steady assuming a stable participation rate), roughly double the pre-COVID level. While the working age population growth will decelerate as the government clamps down on inbound arrivals/visa numbers, we expect this will occur gradually, meaning average employment growth should stay elevated in 2H24 and continue to support aggregate income growth.

## Business survey loses steam

Australia’s NAB business survey for August was soft with the headline confidence and conditions indexes losing ground. Confidence slipped five points to -4 and is now tracking at the lower end of the two-year range. Business conditions, which are more closely correlated with economic activity halved to+3, the lowest reading since the RBA began lifting rates in 2022.

The survey’s labor-related indicators were mixed, but in net terms continue to flag ongoing deterioration in demand. The employment index remains choppy, plunging six points to +1 and unwinding the prior month’s move higher. Using a threemonth moving average to smooth the recent volatility is less alarming, down just one point and little changed from levels earlier in the year. Elsewhere, capacity utilization increased slightly to 82.9%, a level consistent with a gradual increase in the unemployment rate (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Unemployment rate and NAB capacity utilization
![](images/a1076c6753c269cfe1be1bf7d26348181746328c88728ff93e348703b7795e4d.jpg)

There was little to report with respect to the survey’s nominal indicators which, broadly speaking, continue to suggest inflation pressures are easing. Reported prices of final products declined one-tenth to 0.6%q/q and are only slightly above pre-COVID levels. Purchase and labor costs have moderated considerably from recent peaks, but remain elevated versus the long-run average.

Australia Data releases and forecasts Week of September 16 - 20
<table><tr><td colspan="2">Thu Labor force survey</td><td colspan="4"></td></tr><tr><td>Sep 19</td><td></td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Aug</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="3">11:30am</td><td>Unemployment rate (%)</td><td>4..2</td><td>4.1</td><td>4.2</td><td>4.2</td></tr><tr><td>Employment (ch. 000s)</td><td>30.2</td><td>42.1</td><td>58.0</td><td>20.0</td></tr><tr><td>Participation rate (%)</td><td>66.9</td><td>66.9</td><td>67.1</td><td>67.1</td></tr></table>

If our forecast is realized, the US centric Sahm Rule will be triggered which holds that every time the three-month moving average of the unemployment rate is 0.50%-pt or more above the lows of that series over the prior 12 months, then the economy is in, or is about to enter, a recession. The Sahm Rule does not have quite the same track record (at least using US parameters) in Australia and we highlight unemployment remains low and almost a full percentage point below pre-COVID levels. Accordingly, we fade the implications for either monetary policy or the near-term economic trajectory, with the RBA likely to remain on hold for the remainder of the year with cuts likely from early 2025.

Last week, RBA Assistant Governor Hunter delivered a speech on full employment and recent labor market developments. From the RBA’s perspective, the recent increase in the unemployment rate is not unusual given the historical relationship between changes in the unemployment rate, GDP growth and interest rates. The increase in the participation rate has been more surprising with labor supply now at alltime highs. The up trend in female labor force participation, increased employment flexibility (for example, working from home) and strong migration are the obvious drivers and have seen steady state employment double to 40K since 2022. We think the participation rate holds at 67.1% in the August print, but risks are skewed toward another increased based on recent momentum.

<table><tr><td>Thu Real GDP</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Sep 19</td><td>3Q23</td><td>4Q23</td><td>1Q24</td><td>2Q24</td></tr><tr><td>11:30am</td><td>%q/q</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.2 -0.1</td></tr></table>

The NZ economy remains weak, though we think it will have outperformed low RBNZ expectations in the June quarter. We look for a slight contraction of (-0.1%q/q) compared to the more downbeat assessment from the RBNZ’s August MPS of -0.5%q/q. The consumer remained on the back foot through the middle of the year, with retail volumes down more than 1%q/q. Some broader negative payback is also due in this component on residual seasonality from the change in visitor/ travel patterns of recent years, which has tended to boost March quarters and depress June quarters. Inventories should be a strong positive contributor to growth, reflecting steady imports and the slide in consumer goods spending. This is also mirrored in relatively stable manufacturing turnover and the related data on retail and wholesale stock levels. Exports eked out a decent gain, while government spending and private capex likely contracted.

Our forecast implies a less-bad outcome on growth than suggested by some business surveys, though the latter did seem to partially reflect sentiment factors rather than a pure output gauge, given the monthly volatility through 2Q. If our forecast is realized, it will suggest a more moderate deterioration than assumed in the RBNZ staff’s forecast and so moderate the chance of a 50bp cut next meeting (JPM forecast: -25bp).

## Greater China

China: August export activity beat expectations amid sluggish domestic demand

CPI ticked up to 0.6%oya on food prices, underlying deflation pressure intensified

• Credit data in line with expectations, with pick-up in govt bond issuance and softness in loans

• Taiwan: Broad-based strength in August exports

• Next week: Modest sequential upturn in China IP, retail sales and FAI; PBOC and CBC on hold

China’s export sector activity had shown solid growth through 1H24, with net exports of goods and services contributing 0.7%-pt to the 5.0%oya GDP growth. Adjusted for price effects, export volumes grew 13.0%oya in the first seven months, compared to the 4.0% exports value growth in US\$ terms (Figure 1). Following the disappointing July trade report, China’s August exports came in above expectations, with annual growth rate at 8.7%oya, translating to a sequential rebound of 3.9% m/m sa (offsetting the fall of 4.0% in July). Given the latest intensification in PPI deflation pressure, it seems Chinese manufacturers have likely continued to lower export prices to compete in the global market amid sluggish domestic demand. Nominal imports rose a modest 0.5%oya or 1.1% m/m sa, with the trade surplus with the US widening to US\$91.0bn.

Figure 1: Exports decomposition: price vs. volume (in US\$ terms)
![](images/929e36d220b0c584204397283edcde742ac0e79fa85a8171443b13e8309b8f77.jpg)

China’s solid export performance reflects the diversification efforts by Chinese exporters in recent years. By destination, China’s exports have shifted from G3 to EM countries, including EM Asia and in recent years to Russia, LatAm and Africa. While some of the exports may represent trans-shipments to bypass higher US tariffs (e.g., some exports to ASEAN and Mexico), they are also driven by the objective to seek new final demand (e.g., Russia, Africa). Having said that, China’s solid export performance so far this year seems to be rather broadly based across DM and EM markets. The uptrend in the global manufacturing sector and global final

demand, along with the strength of Chinese exports (the scale of production and lower export prices), jointly boosted China’s export sector activity this year.

The rise of protectionism, the wider use of tariffs, export controls, investment restrictions and sanctions, may lead to further distortion or disruption to the global trade system and exert pressure on macro growth. Our global team has noted that the level of global manufacturing PMI output is now below the 50-mark for the first time since December last year, pointing to a stall in factory output, raising downside risk to the outlook for the goods producing sector. While the export sector has been an important growth driver for China in recent quarters, potential slowing in the export engine would imply growing uncertainty on the industrial sector's growth outlook going forward. It reinforces the need for supportive domestic macro policy to stabilize near-term growth.

## Gauging exporters’ USD hoarding

After the drastic unwinding of carry trades involving some low-yield funding currencies (eg. JPY, CNY etc.), the long-lasting gap between official CNY fixing and market spot price has been largely closed since early August. It happened against the backdrop of market repricing of Fed outlook and a hawkish move from the BOJ, which contributed to the drastic position squeeze and unwinding of bearish JPY and CNY short trades. In late August, Bloomberg reported that Stephen Jen estimated Chinese firms may have amassed more than \$2 trillion since the pandemic in offshore investments, and may sell \$1 trillion when the Fed starts rate cuts, which could strengthen CNY by 10%. This has added fuel to market discussions around the scale of Chinese exporters’ USD hoarding and pressure on further CNY strength.

It is straightforward that exporters tend to purchase more or convert less USD when the CNY weakens, but increase USD conversion or reduce USD purchase when CNY strengthens. We take 2015-17 as an example when there was general CNY depreciation pressure. Banks’ FX settlement ratio (on behalf of clients) averaged at 52.7%, 7.6%pts lower than the 2012- 2014 average, and banks’ FX sales ratio averaged at 50.2%, or 2.1%pts higher. Benchmarking to the 2012-2014 average, it suggests Chinese corporates under-settled US\$500.8bn and over-purchased \$225.8bn during 2015-2017. Taken together, it points to \$726.5bn net FX under-settlement.

If we apply a similar exercise to the past 4.5 years, the estimated net FX under-settlement goes to \$1.4 trillion (Figure 2). However, the tricky part is whether 2012-2014 average FX settlement and sales ratios are a good benchmark. Seasonality aside, FX settlement (sales) ratios have trended down (up) a bit in the past decade. Alternatively, if we benchmark to 2015-2019 average, which suggests a 6.4%pts rise for FX settlement ratio and a 6.0% fall for FX sales ratio from July 2024 level, we estimate the FX under-settlement during 2020- 2024 will narrow sharply to US\$230bn. This suggests the underlying assumption of where the equilibrium FX sales and settlement ratios stand matters, and we think its determination should be a dynamic process as the market pricing and expectation of CNY moves.

%, 12-month change over 12-month rolling nominal GDP
Figure 2: Exporters' USD hoarding proxy: vs. historical average US\$ bn, cumulation since Jan 2020
![](images/cad86e6af73df838137e88e430010dfc079d2a0d282d0d86ac1d71f8fe8fea6c.jpg)
Note: Numbers in parentheses are period average of fx settlement, sales ratios.

## Underlying deflation pressure intensified

China’s August CPI inflation rate ticked up further to 0.6%oya (0.2% m/m sa) (Figure 3). Similar to the pattern in July, the uptick was largely led by food prices, while nonfood and core CPI remained soft. Pricing power for consumer goods and services remains soft, reflecting weak momentum in domestic demand (consumer spending and private investment), and a drag from the property market downturn. This is unlikely to change materially in the near term. In particular, amid lingering housing market softness, weakness in house prices and rental costs continues to drag down overall CPI. The rental cost component of the CPI basket remains soft, falling 0.3%oya in August. In addition, consumer confidence remains soft amid lingering concerns of job/income uncertainty, dragging down domestic demand and general pricing conditions for consumer goods and services.

Figure 3: China's inflation dynamics
![](images/1f20aac2656ee1f6c18845c05f8b21082adcb415a9eb203f1bd2e862450647f5.jpg)

PPI stayed in deflationary territory for the 23<sup>nd</sup> consecutive month, falling 1.8%oya in August (compared to the fall of

0.8%oya in July). In sequential terms, PPI fell 0.4% m/m sa, following three consecutive monthly increases through July. The breakdown reports broad-based declines across the energy-related PPI, prices of industrial metals, as well as construction-related input costs.

Our forecast remains for low, positive headline CPI inflation through the rest of the year. That reflects a combination of: (1) a steady recovery in domestic pork prices in recent months which will lift food prices; (2) the low-base effect, which will remain supportive of the annual CPI inflation rate in the coming months (recall that CPI fell into modest deflation zone in 4Q23); and (3) ongoing weakness in general pricing power. Our forecast for full-year 2024 CPI inflation stands at a modest 0.4%. Meanwhile, PPI will likely stay in the deflation zone (in %oya terms) through the year end.

## Credit data in line with expectations

The August TSF and loan data were broadly in line with expectations, but the breakdown suggests that it was mainly supported by the catchup in government bond issuance (Figure 4), while the bank lending channel remains compromised. TSF flow recorded 3030bn yuan and TSF growth edged down to 8.1%oya. New loan creation remained soft, printing at a lower-than-expected 900bn yuan, with loan growth slowing to 8.5%oya. The combination of banks’ weak credit supply incentive, and weak private credit demand have continued to weigh on CNY loan creation. Loans picked up from July, but remained weaker than usual seasonality.

Figure 4: Credit impulse measure and breakdown
![](images/160cbd8879f7adfaf05a4aa7301549b20205d1f9761c86c7eca4b5168988a855.jpg)

Along with yesterday’s August credit report, the PBOC published a statement. It stressed that both TSF growth and yuan loan growth stayed at the above-8%oya pace, higher than 1H nominal GDP growth by about 4%- pt, suggesting accommodative credit support for the real economy. The statement is defensive of the PBOC’s credit policy, yet it may also send an message that the PBOC is tolerant of credit deceleration, and de-emphasizing the importance of credit policy in monetary policy operations. The PBOC officials have recently stressed counter-cyclical monetary policy to support growth, indicating that there is still room to lower RRR (we expect a 25bp

RRR cut in the near term) yet stating the room for further deposit and lending rate cuts is constrained. In our view, “constrained” does not necessarily mean the PBOC will keep policy rates unchanged, instead it may suggest that the PBOC will continue to adopt the so-called “attenuation approach”, with small-step, gradual rate cuts (our forecast: 10bp cut in 4Q24 and another 10bp cut in 1Q25).

## Taiwan: Broad-based strength in exports

Following moderate easing in July, Taiwan’s August export activity came in significantly above expectations, as merchandise exports grew 16.8%oya. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that exports (in volume terms) jumped 9.1% m/m sa in August, following a decline of 3.6% m/m sa in July. Tech exports showed a strong gain of 5.0% m/m sa (or 20.4% 3m/3m saar) (Figure 5), reflecting ongoing strength in AI-related demand, as well as the impact of the upcoming launch of new smartphone models. Non-tech exports have been rather volatile lately, with a sharp rebound of 10.5% m/m sa in August, following the decline of 7.4% in July (partly reflecting a typhoon disruption impact). The merchandise trade surplus rose significantly, to US\$11.5 billion in August, compared to \$4.8 billion in July.

Figure 5: Taiwan export breakdown by products US\$ bn, sa, 3mma
![](images/d1b1dd0a18b8827c00e211ac959c027303ffcd2499f118f76169720f8f24fd4b.jpg)

Our forecasts continue to look for a steady uptrend in Taiwan’s tech exports in the coming quarters. There is also a bounce in Taiwan’s capital goods imports in recent quarters, suggesting a decent recovery in the domestic capex trend and reflecting manufacturers’ constructive outlook on external demand conditions for the coming quarters. Meanwhile, growth concerns on the global economy seem to have risen lately, adding potential risk factors on external demand for Taiwan exports. Nonetheless, if the U.S./global economy manages to avoid a recession (which is embedded in our global team’s soft-landing view), we expect the Taiwan export sector to continue with steady growth, feeding through to decent expansion in domestic economic activity.

## Korea

• Unemployment rate fell further to a record low level in August

• Noisy 10-day exports data for early September; exports volume edged up in August

The data this week bolstered our view that growth momentum should hold up in 2H24. There is currently a disconnect between the unemployment rate and the strength of domestic demand growth. But the seasonally adjusted unemployment rate fell further in August to a record low level, with smart growth in private service employment offsetting a drag in manufacturing employment. The 10-day trade data was noisy from the full-moon holiday timing, yet the trend in export growth is expected to hold up in 3Q.

## Unemployment rate fell to record low

The seasonally adjusted unemployment rate fell 0.1%pt further to 2.4% in August, which is a record low level. The unemployment rate rose to 3.2% back in December 2023, but has trended lower since then. Employment growth has not been particularly strong, with sluggish manufacturing employment in particular. But the labor force is correcting after post-COVID strength, driving the fall in the jobless rate.

Figure 1: Korea labor market
![](images/c301004565ad3bbb6db00b8802d155123b0bc21fe13204c58c8c21f3d02eb5ec.jpg)
Source: NSO, and J.P. Morgan

Total employment was flat in August, with a divergence between sluggish manufacturing and robust service employment (Figure 2). The details suggest that the labor-intensive manufacturing sector (mostly non-tech) is likely shedding labor. Meanwhile new post-COVID job-growth engines, including gig-economy style employment (flexibly hired for online platform businesses) and professional services, are holding up robustly. In all, while some labor shedding is ongoing in the manufacturing sector, there is offsetting growth for private service employment. Meanwhile it appears jobless people have been opting out of the labor force, correcting after the record-high labor force participation ratio seen earlier in February. This has led to downward pressure on the unemployment rate.

Figure 2: Employment growth by industry %3m/3m, sa
![](images/e1b0a81c793e1cd068749b5ca85b45bfacc16efcf66e3b5756d1d3ce70305458.jpg)

The record-low unemployment rate is occurring amid tepid domestic demand conditions. That disconnect partly reflects relatively poor job quality in the new employment growth sectors (temporary hiring contracts and ample labor supplies), so the low unemployment rate has not been pushing up wage growth. In our tracking, nominal wage growth is holding at around a 4% annualized pace, down from the peak in early 2022 (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Wage and core CPI inflation
![](images/05ffb7e7d7f63c6f6089ef780e5f4392a7deb7cdd91c6d33dd3fbc2fedd9a6d2.jpg)

## Real exports grow robustly in 3Q

The high-frequency first 10-day export growth was strong in over-year-ago terms at 24.6%oya. That said, exports the in 10-day data may include front-loading ahead of the lunar-holiday in mid-September (vs the holiday in late-September 2023). The standard seasonal adjustment is often noisy around the lunar holidays, and we see a 3.4%m/m, sa fall in the seasonally adjusted series. More clarity should come from the 20-day data, yet we think the trend growth of customs exports in 3Q should hold up.

The export volume index edged up 0.5%m/m, sa in August if seasonally adjusted by J.P. Morgan, rising 6.7%3m/3m, saar. The import volume index rose 3.4%3m/3m, saar in August, suggesting a continued positive contribution from net exports. While we are awaiting more clarity on domestic demand impulses from the August activity data, external demand conditions are broadly aligned with our view for a smart upturn in overall GDP growth in the current quarter.

## ASEAN

• ASEAN CBs have been susceptible to frequent FX pressures in this cycle given narrow rate buffers

• Against this backdrop, we take stock of the recent rally in the ASEAN FX complex

• Sustained currency strength, to the extent that it brings de-facto tightening, further supports CB easing

Differently from the rest of EM, continued broad-based cooling in EM Asia’s inflation amid enduring slack in the region has strengthened the case for monetary policy easing, particularly in ASEAN. But concerns around FX and financial stability amid narrow rate buffers versus the US has promoted central bank (CB) caution (Figure 1). Indeed, many ASEAN CBs have deployed a combination of FX interventions (FXI), administrative tightening of BoP, stealth tightening via money markets, and even outright rate hikes (Indonesia) to stave off currency pressures at various points over the past two years.

Figure 1: ASEAN-4<sup>\*</sup> rate differential vs US
![](images/1236740909bf38c7a34115450ee3a95028eaf478a1651806227b9a649d168dec.jpg)

Here we take stock of the recent rally in the ASEAN FX complex—the strongest in EM Asia in both US dollar and tradeweighted terms—amid a shift in expectations towards earlier and deeper Fed easing (Figure 2). On the whole, the realized currency appreciation should further mitigate financial stability risks while also raising downside growth risks. This should give CBs in the region more confidence to deliver projected easing, US election risks notwithstanding.

Figure 2: J.P. Morgan policy rate forecast revisions %-pt cumulative revision in year-ahead outlook
![](images/928e9bb9ea13fd7ba95e8e68afb9671ad17a2cb065e546606db625e77e9fdc70.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics

## Bouncing back from April lows

Over the past month, GDP-weighted ASEAN-4 currencies are up 5.1% against USD and 2.8% in trade-weighted terms. This builds on the prior month’s rebound after continued upside US data surprises through April had triggered widespread FX weakness across the region in 2Q (Figures 3, 4).

Figure 3: EM Asia FX vs USD
![](images/475d6d249d5ef5517d64e5b4633fe08e3cccb37473a4cfae2b06e8f49ebe6b68.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, national statistical agencies

Figure 4: EM Asia nominal effective exchange rate (NEER) Index, Jan-22 = 100; higher value = local ccy appreciation
![](images/fef4be95eaf9c40b9d4d71dbcbf828c60c6ed4874bc635f8fb6edd5d35d9b6eb.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, national statistical agencies

The appreciation against USD was significant and broadbased, with Malaysia (+6.3%m/m) leading the pack and the Philippines bringing up the rear (+3.8%m/m). This outperformance also extended to trade-weighted exchange rates. Over the past three months, nominal effective exchange rates (NEER) have strengthened significantly in low-yielding CAS countries (Malaysia, Thailand) and stabilized in high-yielding CAD countries (Indonesia, Philippines; Table 1).

Table 1: FX moves thru Aug-24
<table><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=7>vs USD                      NEER%1m    %3m   %ytd    %1m    %3m    %ytd</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>IDR</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=2>5.2     5.1</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-0.4</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>2.9</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>2.1</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>0.4</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>MYR</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>6.3</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>8.9</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>6.4</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>4.2</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>6.2</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>7.3</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=2 colspan=1>PHPTHB</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>3.8</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>4.3</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-1.4</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>1.4</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>1.0</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>-0.5</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>4.7</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>7.9</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>1.2</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>2.5</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>5.1</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>2.1</td></tr><tr><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>ASEAN-4</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=2>5.1      6.1</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>0.8</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=2>2.8      3.2</td><td rowspan=1 colspan=1>1.6</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan, Haver

Currency moves of such size are usually growth-negative and call for a more accommodative monetary policy. But context matters, and in most cases we see these moves as buying ASEAN CBs valuable insurance against risks of renewed FX weakness from projected rate cuts, though they raise the risk of more front-loaded easing.

## Indonesia: A welcome reprieve

Of all ASEAN economies, Indonesia has been the most sensitive to currency swings in this cycle. Indeed, a sudden bout of IDR weakness amid a drain in net FX reserves had led Bank Indonesia (BI) to deliver a surprise 25bp hike earlier this year (Figure 5), partly on concerns of pass-through to inflation. The roughly 5% rebound in the currency from its April lows amid a return of portfolio inflows and a jump in gross reserves (August net reserves are not yet available), in addition to benign inflation pressures, should bolster BI’s confidence to start cutting in 4Q24.

Figure 5: Indonesia net FX reserves and FASBI rate
![](images/8237c769f1b306a0b87b0f9b48b0f5fdc182f3a0a0e644471f3da2d94ba1d352.jpg)

## Philippines: BSP to stay on easing course

The Philippines should also welcome currency strength. The appreciation against USD mitigates financial stability risks while the modest rebound in NEER should add to the lagged impact on growth from past BSP tightening. Indeed, July trade balance had already posted a significant negative surprise amid a sharp, broad-based uptick in imports, and recent NEER appreciation adds further downside risk. Therefore, BSP should feel comfortable pushing ahead with the rate cutting cycle it started last month, and we look for a 25bp cut in December followed by a 25bp cut at every MPC meeting next year to take the policy rate to 4.25% by end-2025.

## Malaysia: No cuts through end-2025

Unlike Indonesia and the Philippines where current policy rates remain relatively elevated, rates in Malaysia are in line with our estimate of neutral (Figure 6). Growth remains robust—driven by strength in both domestic and external demand, particularly for tech goods—while an anticipated rationalization of the RON95 subsidy poses upside risks to the future path of inflation. While the above-trend growth profile and upside inflation risks should keep BNM on a hawkish tilt, the de-facto tightening from recent appreciation in the ringgit could substitute for policy rate hikes. During the MPC meeting last week, BNM dropped a previous reference to the need to “manage risks from heightened financial market volatility”, indicating that they welcome the recent currency upswing.

![](images/4000813bd595671a6e8f4f53fc57f1b4da07ef462bfa8ed4681c3a56472a64c5.jpg)

## Thailand: NEER strength supports nearterm BoT cuts

The roughly 5% strengthening of Thailand’s trade-weighted exchange rate over the last three months has taken it to its highest level in more than three years, and along with structural post-pandemic issues (loss in export competitiveness and tourism receipts), could weigh on the cyclical recovery in Thai exports and GDP growth. Meanwhile, a continued rise in the stock of non-performing loans (NPLs) has also raised financial stability risks (Figure 7). Last month, we had pulled forward our forecast for BoT rate cuts from 2Q25/3Q25 to 4Q24/1Q25. While currency appreciation constitutes de-facto tightening with negative implications for growth, the bar for additional rate cuts may be high, given our view that the neutral policy rate (2.0%) is higher vis-à-vis pre-pandemic years (around 1.5%) because of a structurally narrower savings-investment gap (see note).

Figure 7: Thailand special mention and non performing loans % of gross loans
![](images/2d34aaa24dbc229a73881523d5c27fbb1fd1ff169d83849f3cb8b0dc65cb5a94.jpg)

## India

• August CPI inflation surprised to the upside ...

• … led by higher-than-anticipated food prices

• IP remained soft driven by weaker mining ...

• … and a subdued recovery in manufacturing

August CPI inflation surprised to the upside on account of food prices, even as core inflation remained soft. Headline CPI is still expected to be 30-40 bps below RBI projections in 3Q and 4Q. However, September CPI inflation is now tracking close to 5% oya, which increases the likelihood that the RBI would like to see more data before it begins to ease. In a recent speech, the RBI governor highlighted that the ‘balance between inflation and growth is well-poised’, suggesting that the RBI may be more comfortable first moving to a ‘neutral stance. While our baseline view is of an October ease, the likelihood has increased that the RBI uses the October meeting to change its stance from ‘withdrawal of accommodation’ to ‘neutral’ and cuts rates in the next (December) meeting. July IP growth declined sequentially due to a sharp drop in mining. Manufacturing showed only modest growth, while consumer durables continued to struggle, pressured by weak auto sales and an inventory build-up.

## Food driven upside CPI surprise

August CPI inflation came in higher than expectations, printing at 3.7% oya (J.P. Morgan 3.3% and Consensus: 3.5%), inching up from 3.6% oya in July (Figure 1). The upward surprise was largely on account of food prices (-0.2% m/m, sa), which declined much less than had been expected.

Figure 1: Headline CPI inflation
![](images/49cc4a3d026050f86b990c9c15b61366edac1692f5d39292e4526d27373084f9.jpg)
Our food price forecasts are based on high frequency Department of Consumer Affairs (DCA) prices. These have typically been a reliable indicator of food inflation in the CPI report, and were pointing to a meaningful and broad-based softening in food prices. In the event, the actual CPI food outturn was significantly higher than DCA prices (Figure 2). For example, in the case of cereals, pulses and oilseeds, DCA prices fell by 1-2% m/m in August but in the CPI report they rose by

0.2-0.6 %. DCA prices continue to point to softer prices in September and we will look closely to see next month whether the August miss was just an issue of leads and lags, in which case September food prices should come off significantly, or whether a divergence is opening up between both series.

Figure 2: August Food Prices DCA vs CPI
![](images/34de88cb3c5af95d2068bfd21ac90aad71b83e16ecc4a2814b5349caf21f87a7.jpg)

Notwithstanding this week’s miss, the food inflation outlook is getting progressively more constructive, something also recently highlighted by the RBI Governor, with monsoon tracking 8.5% above normal, healthy sowing this season and softer global food prices.

Meanwhile, the soft run of core inflation continued in August. Core-core prices rose by just 0.3% m/m, sa – in line with the average over the last six months (Figure 3). On a year-ago basis, core-core inflation was 3.5% in August similar to 3.6% last month. If the recent fall in global commodity prices (particularly oil) is sustained that would place further downward pressure on core inflation.

Figure 3: Core-core prices
![](images/bf455464e9dc937b4887919adf365b26e69e3fe065d71ff9f3985989ae35826a.jpg)
Source: MoSPI, J.P. Morgan

After this week’s upward surprise, September CPI inflation is tracking close to 5% oya, pushed up in part by base effects swinging from favourable to unfavourable. Consequently, headline CPI in 3Q24 is tracking 4% (above our previous estimate of 3.8%) but still below the RBI’s forecast of 4.4%. For 4Q24, we maintain our forecast of 4.3% which is also below the RBI’s forecast of 4.7%.

## IP remains relatively soft

July IP came slightly above expectation at 4.8% oya (J.P. Morgan and Consensus: 4.6%). In sequential terms, IP contracted 0.4% in July on the back of a 0.9% decline in June. The fall in IP was led by mining (-6.3% m/m, sa). As we highlighted earlier, July IP was important to gauge activity after the heat wave and elections. The declines in mining and electricity were expected because the heat wave had induced a jump in coal production and electricity consumption, which are reversing now.

Importantly, the recovery in manufacturing didn’t materialize in July with manufacturing rising by just 0.4% m/m, sa on the back of a 1.4% fall in June. Further, manufacturing production had averaged just 0.1% m/m, sa in 2Q24 vs 0.6% in 1Q24. With July disappointing, it remains to be seen if the recovery materializes in August.

Meanwhile, consumer durables production declined for a second month in a row (-2.3% m/m, sa) likely reflecting softness in auto production, which is facing headwinds from softening retail sales and a large build-up in inventories (Figure 4). Given the extent of the inventory buildup, the risk is consumer durables production will remain under pressure in the coming months.

Figure 4: Consumer Durables & Auto Production
![](images/ad59b9cba14ace540e9ee4bf7396022fa74441b394d48e99c888517357a0c7c7.jpg)
Source: MoSPI, SIAM

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
